QT Group · QTCOM-FI · the new Memo conveyor's card of 11 November 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: QT Group

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 51,801 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The external environment is a weak cyclical headwind turning into a slow structural tailwind. Demand has bottomed, but the recovery is narrow (Europe, China, defence, medical) and unproven in the two lines that drive most of the profit swing: US developer seats and automotive royalties. The structural forces are favourable in direction: certified-software regulation, AI-generated code that needs testing, and cheaper chips that can run rich interfaces all expand QT's market, but each takes 12-36 months to reach revenue and one of them, AI, also threatens the per-seat model. For the modeller, the practical conclusion is that external forces justify a recovery to mid-to-high single-digit organic growth by 2027, but not the 15% organic growth DNB Carnegie models, unless Western automotive R&D restarts; currency is the largest unhedged swing in reported numbers (about 8-10% of 2027 EPS per 10% dollar move), and the external case is more about downside protection (low starting growth, a buyer's floor) than about acceleration.

1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0257C-0560C-0745C-0952C-3555C-3962
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

C+578th of 26

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: competitive intensity is broadly stable in QT's core and slightly easing at the high end, but rising slowly at the low end (microcontrollers and cheap consumer devices), which is exactly where the IAR acquisition is meant to take QT next.

Easing or stable: both covering brokers report no visible change in the competitive environment and no serious new threat in safety-critical work. Google's Flutter is reported (Q2 2026 Earnings Review, citing the company) to have moved its embedded effort into maintenance mode; Unity has made no inroads into automotive user interfaces and caused no price pressure; specialist tools such as Rightware's Kanzi and Elektrobit's EB GUIDE are losing momentum as suppliers standardise; HTML5 and Electron are too resource-hungry for embedded hardware. Android Automotive holds the infotainment screen, but not the safety-certified instrument cluster.

Rising: the open-source libraries LVGL and Slint are light, free, easy for AI coding agents to use, and growing in consumer internet-of-things devices on microcontrollers. QT answers with tooling (Qt for MCUs, Qt Design Studio, the new AI design tool Clate) and silicon partnerships (Infineon PSOC Edge, Nuvoton Gerda, Qualcomm), and the company itself published a "Qt for MCUs vs LVGL" comparison in May 2026, which shows it takes the threat seriously.

The interaction that matters: the low end is the growth market, and QT's own price increases (5-10% a year on developer licences, larger in past restructurings) have pushed some small and mid-sized customers away (an expert account from 2023). Intensity is therefore a function of QT's own pricing as much as of rivals. In testing, competition is fragmented and QT is a consolidator; in compilers, IAR faces free chip-vendor and open-source toolchains (GCC, LLVM) but holds a certified, safety-critical niche.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

A602nd of 26

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: QT has almost no supplier exposure; its "input cost" is people, and wage inflation has eased, so the net effect is a mild tailwind in 2026-2027, helped by the restructuring; the only supply-side risk that matters is customers' own component supply, which acts on royalties.

QT's direct material and service costs are small (external services and subcontracting EUR 5.3 million in 2025; IT costs EUR 6.9 million in 2024), hosting is with AWS and Equinix-type data centres, and no vendor has pricing power over it. Personnel is about 64% of operating costs and is anchored in euros and Swedish kronor. DNB Carnegie reports that wage inflation in IT has levelled off; QT has cut up to 200 roles (about 18% of the pre-cut workforce), moved work from Finland (headcount there down 28.7% to 210) to lower-cost European and Asian hubs, and expects at least EUR 20 million of annual savings in 2027. Average headcount still rose 10.4% to 1,011 in Q3 2026 because IAR was added, and personnel costs rose 38.4% year on year; the cost base therefore falls only from 2027.

The indirect supply risk is in customers' supply chains: chip shortages or factory stoppages delay device shipments and cut distribution royalties even when designs are finished. With automotive semiconductor inventories still high (167 days and rising, per the Infineon digest), the risk now is weak ordering rather than shortage.

Two cost risks to watch: management says the savings will partly be reinvested (APAC sales, AI research), so the full EUR 20 million will not drop to profit; and cloud and AI token costs would rise if QT embeds more AI into its products, a small but growing line.

C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

C457th of 26

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: macro bites hardest at the company level, through three channels the sector does not share to the same degree: Western automotive and industrial capital spending, the dollar, and the small-company discount in a risk-off market.

Company level, strongest: (1) Western automotive and industrial weakness (light-vehicle production down 2% globally in Q3 2026, North America down 5%; European and US carmakers cutting R&D) acts twice, on developer seats with a lag and on royalties directly. (2) Currency: 62% of revenue is in foreign currencies against 32% of costs, with no hedging. The dollar's fall cost 3.1 points of growth in 2025 (EUR 6.1 million) and EUR 3.3 million in H1 2026, while forecasters' path for the next 12-24 months (EUR/USD around 1.15-1.16) is roughly flat. Our own arithmetic, on a 2027 earnings base: a 10% fall in the dollar takes about EUR 8.7 million from revenue and, after about EUR 3.4 million of US-dollar costs, about EUR 5 million from EBITA, or roughly 8-10% of 2027 EPS; the Risks report's figure of 23% applies to the depressed 2025-2026 earnings base. (3) Interest rates: the US ten-year above 5% and a risk-off regime hurt small, long-duration equities; QT's own interest bill is small (about EUR 4-5 million in 2026) and falling as debt is repaid.

Sector level, moderate: European business software fell as one on AI fears and is coming back stock by stock; the Fitness for Fighting digest moved the Sector to Neutral from Underweight as money rotates into software, but holds small and fragile companies at Double Underweight.

Industry level, weakest: the technology industry's rotation (semiconductors falling, software recovering) is a mild help.

The euro-area economy (growth about 0.8% in 2026, inflation 2.7%) is slow but not the main driver; QT's macro exposure is to global device manufacturing and US-dollar pricing.

C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–52

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: three other external dynamics matter: a buyer's floor under European software valuations (positive), the market's habit of de-rating perceived "AI losers" regardless of results (negative), and the dollar's longer-term direction (negative skew).

  1. Private and strategic buyers. Schneider Electric agreed to buy PTC for USD 22.6 billion at 21 times 2027 EBITA; Siemens paid 31 times EBITDA for Altair; private-equity dry powder is USD 1.3 trillion and sponsors underwrite software exits at 15-20 times EBITDA. QT trades at about 10 times 2027 consensus EBITDA, its register is mostly open (free float about 66%; the Ingman group holds 21.6%), and it is named in the Sector digest among the widely held names cheapest against private benchmarks. This is a floor argument, not a catalyst: a bid would need the Ingman group's support.
  2. The AI-loser de-rating. Innoscripta, with 60% margins and 43% growth, fell 15% in H1 2026 and is down 55% over twelve months, because the market fears AI will commoditise its service. QT was caught in the same trade in early 2026 (Inderes: AI fears pushed software multiples to cyclical lows) and the risk can return with any new AI coding release, whatever QT reports.
  3. Currency. Most forecasters see EUR/USD at 1.15-1.16 over 12-24 months, but Morgan Stanley's French-election tail has the euro weaker (1.08 by end-2027, a help to QT) while a Deutsche Bank path to 1.25 would hurt; the asymmetry for an unhedged euro-cost exporter is negative if the dollar keeps weakening.
  4. Smaller items: the Finnish sovereign's debt dynamics were flagged by Goldman Sachs but are not material to QT's revenue; the cost of debt (European high yield 90 basis points wider since September) matters only for buyers' financing of any bid.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

C-5510th of 26

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: adjacent entrants are a low and falling threat; standard entrants are a low threat; orthogonal entrants, AI-native tools that generate user-interface code directly or make free libraries as easy to use as Qt, are the only material threat, and it is rising but unproven.

  1. Adjacent entrants (low, falling). Big-technology platforms (Google with Flutter and Android Automotive, gaming engines such as Unity, cloud providers' open-source frameworks) have tried and mostly stepped back; chipmakers prefer to partner with QT rather than build user-interface toolchains, because they sell silicon (verdict in the Business Foundations report: unlikely, five-year-plus horizon). Large carmakers occasionally consider in-house frameworks, but maintenance costs limit this.
  2. Standard entrants (low). New commercial GUI vendors face the scale threshold (about USD 100 million of ARR to fund global sales and certification) and the developer-talent problem; none is visible.
  3. Orthogonal entrants (medium, rising). Three forms: (a) AI design-to-code tools that turn a visual design straight into code, bypassing Qt Design Studio (former QT vice-president, 2024); (b) AI coding agents that write raw C or LVGL code well enough for simple devices, which lowers the cost of the free alternative; (c) AI-native competitors with new monetisation models. QT's response, Clate and AI assistants across its tools, is the right one; whether it keeps Qt as the platform under AI-generated code or becomes a commodity library is the long-term question.

The threat is concentrated in the segments where the low-end growth is, which makes it more important than its current size suggests.

C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

A622nd of 26

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: suppliers are not material as a source of risk; the companies that look like suppliers (chipmakers) are in fact partners and a support.

QT's own inputs are people, cloud hosting (AWS, Equinix-type data centres) and standard IT; no supplier has pricing leverage, and accounts payable were only EUR 4.0 million in 2025. Chipmakers do not sell to QT; they pre-integrate Qt and IAR tools into their microcontrollers and system-on-chips to speed their own customers' development, which generates leads for QT at no cost. The new partnerships of 2025-2026 (Qualcomm Dragonwing, Infineon PSOC Edge, NVIDIA Halos, Nuvoton Gerda) show this relationship strengthening. The only supply-side risk is indirect: shortages or factory stoppages at customers' component suppliers delay device shipments and therefore royalties. A small future risk is AI-model token costs if QT embeds generative AI more deeply in its tools; management has not quantified it.

1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0158C-0352C-0462C-0860C-3258C-3764C-4366
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

F5820th of 26

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: demand is improving cyclically from a 2025 trough, but unevenly and with low visibility; the structural demand line (more screens, more embedded software, more safety and security certification) is intact and is slowly strengthening.

The 2025 slowdown was mostly cyclical. Customers delayed new product development, bought fewer developer seats, moved from 3-year to 1-year contracts and postponed deals worth up to EUR 5 million each. Organic growth fell to 2.6%, the lowest since 2014, against 18.5% in 2023 and a 2019-2024 compound rate of about 29%. Three facts show that the base did not break: customers renewed rather than left (stable, low churn; seat reductions happen when a product moves into maintenance); organic ARR, excluding IAR, still grew 11.5% in Q2 2026; and Q3 2026 constant-currency growth was 26%, led by European developer licence sales to mid-sized and large customers.

Over the next six months the pattern is split. Automotive is strong in China and Asia but weak at Western carmakers (global light-vehicle production fell 2% in Q3 2026, North America 5%, with a further 4% fall expected in Q4). Defence and aerospace are particularly active, medical is stable, and industrial automation and consumer electronics remain soft. The United States is the weakest region (an execution as well as a demand problem). The structural drivers are real but slow: software-defined vehicles, the move of rich user interfaces down to cheap microcontrollers, AI-generated code that needs more testing, and the EU Cyber Resilience Act.

Visibility is low. Sales cycles run up to 18 months, about 95% of a contract's value is recognised when it is signed, distribution royalties follow customers' production, and Q4 carries the year. No part of the cyclical upswing has two or more years of visibility, so it does not qualify as a visible cyclical swing. For the model: treat 2027 organic growth of 6-9% as a recovery assumption, not a contracted one; ARR (organic +11.5%) is the best leading indicator because it is not distorted by upfront recognition.

C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

D5215th of 26

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the disruption force is generative AI in software development; it is strengthening, it acts in both directions, and so far the evidence disproves the negative case more than it proves it, while the positive case is only beginning to be monetised.

Negative direction (seat compression): AI coding tools let teams produce the same output with fewer engineers. DNB Carnegie's illustration is a customer with 100 licences needing 90-95. Since 95% of developer licence revenue is per seat and renewals are where seat counts are reset, a 5-10% seat reduction would quietly take 3-6% off group revenue. A former QT vice-president warned in 2024 that AI design-to-code tools threaten Qt Design Studio. The Risks report's least-monitored failure mode is exactly this.

Positive direction: AI multiplies code volume and complexity, and safety-critical code (automotive, medical, defence) still needs certified tools and human-governed verification, which raises demand for testing and static analysis (Squish, Axivion, Coco) and for certified compilers (IAR). Qt's open-source code is widely present in AI training data, so models write Qt code well, which keeps Qt the default framework for AI-assisted teams. QT is turning this into product (Clate generates QML code from designs; AI assistants across the tools; Axivion checks NVIDIA CUDA code against safety rules) and into a pricing change (a gradual move from per-seat towards capacity, usage and compliance-based pricing, starting with new customers in test markets).

Evidence so far: management reports that seat counts are unaffected and churn is low; organic ARR grew 11.5%; developer licences grew in Europe in Q3 2026. That disproves a fast collapse, but not a slow erosion, which would show first as smaller renewals rather than churn. Nothing yet proves the upside either: management told analysts not to model revenue from the new pricing. The direction for 2027-2029 depends on whether QT changes the unit it charges for before seat counts shrink, which is the Sector digest's central test for software vendors.

C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

C+628th of 26

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: regulation is a moderate, strengthening tailwind (security and functional-safety rules raise demand for certified, commercially supported tools), while trade policy is a moderate headwind that acts through customers' production volumes and decision speed rather than directly.

Tailwinds: the EU Cyber Resilience Act's reporting obligations began in September 2026, with full application from December 2027. Qt 6.12 is the first Qt release carrying a signed EU Declaration of Conformity, and management and Inderes expect the Act to push open-source Qt users towards paid commercial licences, because a commercially supported, documented framework is the cheapest route to compliance. Industrial customers such as Kontron already use Axivion for the Act and IEC 62443. The EU Product Liability Directive (transposition by 9 December 2026), functional-safety standards (ISO 26262, IEC 61508, IEC 62304) and European driver-monitoring and safety mandates all raise the value of certified tools and testing. European CO2 fleet rules push cheaper electric cars with digital cockpits.

Headwinds: US tariffs and US-China friction slowed customer decisions in 2025 and reduced device production, which cuts royalties; Western automakers face tariff-driven cost increases of 15-25% on exports to North America; export-control compliance is a cost but not a material risk. The Digital Omnibus deferred high-risk AI deadlines to December 2027 and regulated machinery to August 2028, which removes no QT demand but delays some.

The regulatory force is slow to show in revenue: conversions from open source happen at product launch or renewal, so the effect builds over 2027-2028. It is a real, dated driver (a "make the money before the date" mandate in the Industry digest's language) and the CRA's December 2027 date gives a natural pull-forward of compliance purchases into 2027.

C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

F6019th of 26

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: two industry-level drivers are accelerating demand at the margin (the shift of compliance and safety rules into software, and AI-driven growth in code that must be tested), but the largest volume driver, more screens per vehicle and device, is not accelerating in 2026; it is held back by weak Western vehicle production.

Accelerating: (1) Security and functional-safety compliance (EU Cyber Resilience Act, Product Liability Directive, ISO 26262 and IEC standards, defence FACE conformance) lifts demand for commercially licensed Qt, for Axivion and Coco and for IAR's certified compilers; it is the driver management and Inderes framed as stronger than expected in Q3 2026. (2) AI code generation raises the volume of code to verify, the case for the testing business's 20%-a-year growth and its EUR 100 million ambition (from EUR 34.5 million in 2024). (3) Edge-AI devices and RISC-V adoption in Asia raise demand for certified microcontroller toolchains (IAR) and for user interfaces on cheap chips.

Not accelerating: software-defined vehicles and digital cockpits are spreading (Bernstein; content per vehicle rising), but S&P Global expects automotive unit sales to grow only 1.4% in 2027, so content gain must do all the work, and royalties lag design wins by three to five years. Western carmakers are delaying new programmes.

The net is a slow acceleration, concentrated in the smaller lines (testing, compliance-driven conversions) and in Asia, rather than in the core developer seat and royalty volumes that set most of the revenue. The model should let testing and compliance grow faster than the group (15-20%) while holding core developer licence growth to mid-single digits until Western automotive R&D recovers.

C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

D5817th of 26

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: customer behaviour supports the thesis on retention and pricing, and only partly on volume; customers are staying, renewing at higher prices and starting to buy again in Europe and Asia, but they buy fewer seats, for shorter periods and in smaller deals. The support is fragile to a second leg of Western industrial weakness.

Supportive: low churn and stable renewals; customers renew because Qt is embedded in their products; ARR (organic) +11.5% in Q2 2026; European developer licence sales strong in Q3 2026 with more mid-sized and large wins; Chinese carmakers launching new electric models on Qt; defence, aerospace and medical active; new customers such as SAUTER (building automation) and consumer-electronics design wins that will feed royalties when products ship.

Unsupportive: cost-conscious buying: the minimum viable seat count, 1-year rather than 3-year terms, initial deals of EUR 50,000-100,000 that expand later, and large deals (up to EUR 5 million) postponed; Western carmakers in budget and hiring freezes; US customers cautious; industrial automation delaying factory projects.

The thesis needs customers to restart new product programmes, not just renew existing ones, because programmes drive both seat growth and, three to five years later, royalties. The current evidence is that renewals are robust and new programmes are restarting in Europe and Asia but not yet in the US or in Western automotive.

Fragility: high to the macro cycle (a downturn shows within one or two quarters in deal size and timing), low to competition (customers do not leave). For the model, the downside case is not churn but another year of seat-count and deal-size compression.

C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

B+645th of 26

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: regulators help (security, product liability and functional-safety rules favour certified, commercially supported tools), political actors hurt mildly (tariffs and US-China friction cut customer production and slow decisions), and the helpful position is robust because the rules are law with dates, while the harmful one is cyclical.

Helping: the EU Cyber Resilience Act (reporting from September 2026, full application December 2027), the Product Liability Directive (transposition by December 2026), the EU AI Act (full enforcement from August 2026, high-risk deadlines December 2027), ISO 26262 and IEC 61508/62304, Euro NCAP driver monitoring, and defence standards (FACE). Each raises the value of documentation, certification and testing, which is QT's product. EU CO2 fleet rules accelerate cheaper electric cars with digital cockpits.

Hurting: US tariffs (15-25% cost increases for carmakers exporting to North America), US-China decoupling and Chinese self-sufficiency drives (which could, over time, favour local tools in China, where QT has about 13% of revenue); trade uncertainty delays new launches.

Fragility: the helpful rules could be softened or delayed (the Digital Omnibus already deferred some AI deadlines), and customers could build compliance wrappers around free software (an expert view in the Change Forces report), but the direction of regulation in safety and security is one-way. The political headwind would intensify only if tariffs triggered an industrial recession, which would act through C-01 and C-07.

C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

C6611th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: the realistic market is large relative to QT: about USD 5-10 billion for embedded development, user-interface and quality tools, of which QT has about 3-5%, plus a USD 2-3 billion microcontroller tool market growing about 10% a year. It allows high single-digit to low double-digit organic growth over the mid term; it does not constrain growth, demand timing does.

Components (Business Foundations report unless stated): embedded software development tools, HMI design and software quality assurance, USD 5-10 billion; the adjacent microcontroller tool market opened by IAR, USD 2-3 billion growing about 10% a year; testing and quality assurance, which management wants to grow from EUR 34.5 million (2024) to EUR 100 million (20% a year implies about six years). The chief executive has framed the potential of the present product portfolio at EUR 400-500 million of revenue, roughly double today's, with longer-term ambitions "well beyond doubling or tripling". Software-defined vehicles are growing at an estimated 20%-plus a year, but vehicle volumes grow about 1-2%.

The realistic growth rate the market allows: market growth of perhaps 7-10% a year (more screens, more embedded software, more compliance), plus QT's price increases (about 5% a year on developer licences), less seat pressure from AI, plus share gains at the low end and in testing. That supports organic growth of 8-12% a year in normal conditions, consistent with management's new aim of about 10% and with consensus growth of 8% in 2027-2028, which looks cautious against the market but realistic against 2025-2026 demand.

1c

Overall external picture

2 answers
C-0660C-1058
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

D6016th of 26

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the company's own cycle bottomed in Q2-Q3 2025 and is turning up, but the turn in reported revenue overstates the turn in the organic business; organic revenue is in an early, shallow recovery, while ARR and European bookings are turning more convincingly.

The cycle: growth slowed from 28% in 2022 to 16% in 2023-2024 and 3.5% in 2025, with quarterly revenue falling year on year in Q2 and Q3 2025 (-3.9% and -3.4%). The turn: Q4 2025 +12.6% (a strong close with large distribution licence deals), then +11.6%, +19.6% and +27.0% in 2026. But IAR adds about EUR 8 million a quarter, and currency was negative in H1. On the files' figures, organic growth at constant currency was low single digits in H1 2026 (about 3% in Q2) and roughly mid-single digits in Q3.

Three lines move at different stages of the cycle:

  • Developer licences (about 60% of revenue): turning, led by Europe; the US is still weak.
  • Distribution royalties (about 25%): still cyclical and lagging; down 17.4% in H1 2026 and 32.4% in Q2 against a record Q2 2025, up 18.2% in Q3, which management called a timing benefit. Management expects royalties to be flat or slightly lower over the medium term. Consensus has them about 9% lower in 2026 (EUR 51.9 million against EUR 56.8 million).
  • IAR: in a deliberate trough (subscription bookings 68% in Q2 against a 40% plan; revenue down 20% in 2026 on DNB Carnegie's estimate) and set to return to growth in 2027 (FactSet segment consensus EUR 33.0 million, 39.6 million, 47.4 million for 2026-2028).

The implied Q4 2026 matters for the model: consensus of EUR 243 million for 2026 implies Q4 revenue of about EUR 77 million, flat on Q4 2025 and roughly flat organically. The guidance floor implies only about EUR 72 million. The revenue turn is therefore not yet tested against the hardest comparison, and Q4 2026 is the first quarter where IAR is in both years.

C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

C-5813th of 26

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the external picture is mildly positive and improving: the headwinds (Western automotive and industrial weakness, the dollar, the small-cap discount) are cyclical and fading slowly, while the tailwinds (compliance and safety regulation, AI-driven testing demand, edge-AI and microcontroller adoption, software consolidation bids) are structural but slow to reach revenue. The single largest external swing is the timing of Western automotive and industrial R&D recovery.

By thematic driver, as the Sector and Industry digests frame them:

  • How the vendor charges under AI (Sector theme). Mixed for QT. Royalties (per device) and the planned capacity and compliance pricing are on the winning side; developer seats, about 60% of revenue, are on the exposed side. Net: neutral to slightly positive, conditional on the pricing change being made in 2027-2028.
  • Dated mandates and forced migration (Industry theme). Positive: the Cyber Resilience Act (December 2027) and safety rules convert open-source users and pull testing demand forward; IAR's subscription "exit date" is 2027. The Industry digest names QT's 2027 exit date as still early, that is, not yet priced.
  • A buyer's floor (Sector and Industry theme). Positive as protection, not as a driver.
  • Sector rotation and disruption de-rating. The Sector fell as one and recovers company by company; QT was paid for proving its trough shallow (shares up 76% in six months to October), then fell about 16% after its Q3 results, so the market's reward depends on the next proof.
  • Value-chain and macro. Negative but fading: Western automotive, US weakness, tariffs and the dollar.

Value-chain level: customers' R&D budgets are the binding constraint, silicon partners are a support, and suppliers are irrelevant. Industry level: software consolidation and AI are net positive. Sector level: risk-off and small-company fragility are a headwind to the multiple rather than to earnings.

All together: external forces will probably add little to 2026-2027 revenue growth on their own (we would model organic growth of mid-single digits in 2026 and 6-9% in 2027, recovering further only if Western automotive R&D restarts) but they carry more upside than downside over three years, because compliance-driven and AI-driven demand compounds while the cyclical drag cannot fall much further from a 2.6% organic year.

Internal change forces/risks

–not yet rated

Memo topic 2, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Internal change forces

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

This is a classic profit-first reset by an owner-backed, long-tenured chief executive after a growth shock, executed fast on costs and slowly on demand. The corporate change force is genuinely strong in the part of the P&L management controls: costs (EUR 20 million of savings, up to 200 roles), the deliberate IAR revenue trough that ends in 2027, the end of restructuring charges, and conservative guidance that has rebuilt some credibility. It is weaker where valuation is decided: organic growth, the US, the testing unit's leadership and public measurement of all three. The Wisdom Library pattern that fits best is a transformation play with capacity to suffer: management accepted visible damage to 2026 revenue (IAR) and earnings (restructuring) for a stronger 2027, and the two conditions that make that a positive signal are both present (a leading indicator, ARR and subscription bookings, already moving; and a long-horizon owner and a chief executive with real equity). The main corporate risk is not strategy but execution strain below the top two people. For the modeller: the corporate changes make the 2027 margin step-up likely, its size the open question; they do not justify assuming a return to the 15-25% growth rates of 2019-2024.

2a

Internal risks

6 answers
Judgement45C-1464C-8638C-5952C-8148D-0360
Group judgement

Risks

C+456th of 26

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The risk profile is dominated by cyclicality and leverage of the operating kind, not by balance-sheet or structural collapse risk: the organic engine is running at about a quarter of its normal speed, royalties are still falling, the cost base is fixed in the short run, and currency is unhedged, so a second downturn would hit earnings hard. Structural risks (AI seats, low-end open source) are real but slow, and the main share-price risk is sentiment. The Risks report's conclusion that a "false friend" profile wins its competing-hypotheses test should be read with care: the Wisdom Library notes that the vaguest hypothesis tends to win a fewest-contradictions contest, and the false-friend hypothesis here ("headline growth masks seat compression") is supported by the inorganic distortion of reported growth but not by any evidence of seat loss at renewal. The evidence supports a narrower statement: reported growth overstates organic growth (true), and seat compression is a monitored risk (unproven). Downside is meaningful (to EUR 17-22 in a bear case) but bounded by low valuation, falling debt and a plausible buyer's floor. For the modeller: run a bear case of 2% organic growth, royalties -15% and a dollar 10% weaker, which takes 2027 EBITA to about EUR 60-65 million; the base case should not assume the risks away, but should not centre on them either.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

B646th of 26

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: standards are improving clearly in cost discipline and forecasting caution, and somewhat in disclosure; execution in the US and in forecasting large deals is still unproven.

Improving:

  • Cost discipline. A reorganisation launched on 20 April 2026 cuts up to 200 roles (56 in Finland, the US done by June, other European countries through late 2026), at a cost of EUR 9.7 million in the first nine months and for at least EUR 20 million of annual savings in 2027. Headcount fell from 1,120 at end-March to 1,034 at end-June 2026. IAR's embedded security business was closed (EUR 3 million of costs, almost no revenue).
  • Forecasting. After missing internal forecasts for three quarters in 2025 (the chief executive said the sales forecasting models were "broken"), the company rebuilt its forecasting and moved to floor-only guidance. Two quarters (Q2 and Q3 2026) beat consensus on revenue by 7-8%.
  • Disclosure. ARR has been published quarterly since Q4 2025 and distribution licence revenue quarterly since early 2026; an investor-relations lead is being hired.
  • Accounting. IAR's capitalisation of R&D was stopped in 2026 so that group accounting is consistently conservative (only EUR 0.14 million capitalised in 2025).
  • Governance. Every board member is independent of the company; the board used its clawback right in 2025; the chief executive's 2025 pay was entirely fixed because no bonus was earned.

Not yet improved: organic growth, segment revenue and contract duration are still undisclosed; the US is still the weakest region after a year of fixes; the 2024 and 2025 misses were caused by large deals slipping across quarter ends, a risk that floor guidance hides rather than removes; employee turnover rose from 11.0% (2024) to 12.1% (2025), before the 2026 cuts.

C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

D3810th of 26

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the main negatives are the risk that the cost cuts damage execution in the growth units, the persistent US sales problem, the risk that pricing alienates the developer community at the low end, and the opacity that lets inorganic and contract-mix effects mask the organic trend.

Present:

  • Execution strain. Up to 200 roles cut (about 18% of the pre-cut workforce) in the same year as an integration, a SaaS conversion at IAR and leadership changes in the IAR and testing units. Turnover was already rising (12.1% in 2025); former employees describe a centralised, top-down culture where key engineering and sales talent has left before.
  • US sales. Management calls the US a clear area for improvement after more than a year; it is about 34% of revenue.
  • Savings partly reinvested. Management's own warning that the EUR 20 million is not a straight cut in the 2027 cost base caps the margin upside.
  • Opacity. Reported growth (+19% in nine months) is mostly IAR and currency; maintenance revenue up 155-165% is IAR's installed base, not new demand. The false-friend risk in the Risks report is real in this specific sense: the reported numbers flatter the organic trend.

Plausible:

  • Pricing backlash. Aggressive price rises push small customers and hobbyist-to-commercial conversions towards LVGL and Slint, which narrows the funnel of future royalties.
  • IAR churn. If perpetual-licence customers resist subscriptions, IAR's 2027 recovery would slip; so far there is no sign of it.
  • Incentives. The 2025-2027 share plan pays nothing unless 2027 revenue exceeds EUR 333 million; it is therefore almost certainly worthless, which weakens retention of key people, while the chief executive's bonus is tied to organic growth that the company does not disclose.
  • Forecast credibility. A third miss in four years (after 2022 and 2025) would reset the market's trust; floor guidance makes this unlikely in 2026 but does not protect 2027 consensus.
C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

D5216th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement: partly congruent. Goals, strategy and product execution align around the developer-toolchain vision, but three tensions run through the system: incentives weighted to revenue while strategy now emphasises margin; aggressive commercial enforcement against an open-source community the business depends on; and a centralised culture in a company whose growth depends on decentralised sales in three regions.

Congruent elements: a long-term owner and a chief executive with real equity; conservative accounting and guidance; a single toolchain strategy; a Culture Handbook (October 2025), pulse surveys three times a year, Code of Conduct training (92% completion); flat structures where staff feel able to speak (former employees).

Tensions:

  1. Incentives. The chief executive's bonus is tied to organic net sales growth and an operating-profit threshold; experts describe a top-line-weighted compensation culture that drove sales turnover. The 2025-2027 share plan rewards revenue above EUR 333 million, which is out of reach.
  2. Open source. Qt's commercial funnel starts with free users; compliance-based selling and price rises have created friction in that community.
  3. Centralisation. Strategic decisions sit with the chief executive and CFO; board and management meetings are often in Finnish; international sales leadership turns over.

The Lifco or Avanza standard of congruence is not met; QT's system is coherent in strategy but uneven in people and incentives.

C-81

What fundamental risks are there that are sudden, high-magnitude and low-probability, and how plausible and how large is each?

C+488th of 26

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: five such risks matter; none is likely, but two (a synchronised industrial recession and an AI-driven step-down in seats) would each take 25-40% off 2027 EBITA, and the company has no hedges, segment disclosure or contract structure that would warn investors early.

Our estimates over 18 months, with the reasoning:

  1. Synchronised automotive and industrial recession (for example a tariff escalation). Royalties fall 25-35% (Q2 2026 showed -32% against a high base), developer seat purchases freeze, large deals slip. Plausibility about 15%. Impact: EUR 15-25 million of revenue and, through operating leverage, 25-40% of EBITA. The 2025 precedent (EBITA -27% on a growth shortfall of about 12 points) calibrates it.
  2. AI-driven seat step-down. A new generation of coding agents leads large customers to cut seats 10-15% at renewal over 12-24 months, before capacity pricing is in place. Plausibility about 10%. Impact: 6-10% of developer licence revenue, 15-25% of EBITA, plus a sector-style de-rating. Early warning: falling average contract value with stable deal counts.
  3. A dollar collapse (EUR/USD at 1.30-1.35 against about 1.16 today). Plausibility about 5-10%. Impact: on our arithmetic about 13-15% of 2027 EPS for a 15% move; the Risks report's figures are higher because they use the depressed 2025-2026 base.
  4. A major security vulnerability in the Qt framework under the new EU product-liability and Cyber Resilience Act regime, which would hit the commercial argument of "certified and supported" just as it becomes the main selling point. Plausibility low (about 2-3%), impact on reputation and conversions moderate to high; not discussed in the files, so it is our own hypothesis.
  5. Key-person loss. The chief executive runs a centralised model with no visible successor; plausibility about 3-5%; impact mainly on the multiple and on M&A strategy.

Lower-impact or slower risks (not sudden): IAR subscription churn (10%; would delay the 2027 IAR recovery by a year), a carmaker-backed open-source framework, and Q4 deal slippage (a recurring 20-25% risk each year, which floor guidance cushions).

The Risks report's 35-40% "failure probability" combines these with gradual risks; we would put the probability that 2027 EBITA lands more than 20% below consensus at about 20-25%.

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

C+602nd of 6

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the drivers are concentrated, in three different ways that pull predictability in different directions. First, the change from 2026 to 2027 is concentrated in three self-help items that management controls and has largely executed, which is good for predictability. Second, growth from 2027 to 2030 is concentrated in one franchise and one mechanism, the price escalator on developer licences, which is steady but slow. Third, the variance around any single year is concentrated in three things management does not control (the timing of fourth-quarter deals, royalties and the dollar) and in one quarter: on my corrected figures the fourth quarter earns about half of 2026's underlying EBITDA and about 42% of 2027's, more than the 39% the second set stated.

What differs from the last four to six years. Growth falls from about 29% a year (2019-2024) to 7-8%; the margin returns to its 2023-2024 level instead of rising to a new one; cash accumulates instead of being spent on hiring and acquisitions. The drivers of the historical growth (the move from perpetual to subscription licences, the cut in multi-year discounts, the 2021-2023 pulse of customer programmes, headcount-led expansion) are absent from the forecast. The forecast does not need any of them to recur, which removes a class of error.

How concentrated each part of the change is.

Change Size Where it comes from
Reported EBITA, 2026 to 2027 about +26 End of restructuring charges +9.7; savings not yet in the 2026 base about +10; IAR about +4.5; price on developer licences about +7; volume, testing, conversions and royalties about +5; wage inflation, reinvestment, bonuses and other costs about -10
Revenue, 2027 to 2030 +63.5 Developer licences, maintenance and consulting excluding IAR +42.7 (67%), of which price about +24 and volume, testing and new customers about +19; IAR +14.1 (22%); royalties +6.6 (10%)
EBITDA, 2027 to 2030 +33.6 An incremental margin of about 53% on that revenue

In 2027 the three self-help items (the charge ending, the savings, IAR) together equal about 93% of the rise in reported EBITA, with price paying for cost inflation and reinvestment. From 2028 about two thirds of growth comes from one line and close to 40% from its price escalator alone.

Diffuse where it helps. More than 3,500 customers, none above 10% of revenue, in about 70 industries: no single customer decision moves the forecast. Concentrated where it hurts. All product lines depend on device makers' R&D budgets, 62% of revenue is in foreign currencies with nothing hedged, and the profit is earned in one quarter in which the largest contracts are signed in the last days.

Concentration of the difference from consensus. It is in one line. Consensus royalties are EUR 57.8 million and EUR 64.2 million for 2027 and 2028, against our EUR 52.5 million and EUR 54.6 million, at a margin near 100%. Royalties account for more than our whole EBITDA shortfall against consensus in both years; we are above consensus on the rest of revenue (D-01, point 4).

What the record of comparable forecasts shows (AlphaSense report on drivers and dispersion). At QT the item that failed was always revenue timing: growth guidance was missed in each of 2022-2025 because large deals slipped (about EUR 8 million in the fourth quarter of 2024 alone, per DNB Carnegie), while margin guidance was met in 2023 and 2024. Cerence's forecasts rested on a royalty recovery that did not come. Nemetschek, with 95% recurring revenue, met revenue consensus in ten of eleven quarters. The lesson for QT: a forecast concentrated in costs and price has been deliverable; a forecast concentrated in royalties or in year-end deals has not.

The useful consequence. Because the 2027 change is concentrated in the cost base, most of it can be verified early. By the first-quarter 2027 report (late April 2027) the year's cost run-rate will be known to within about 2%; from then on the open item is revenue, a third of which arrives in the fourth quarter.

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

F5021st of 26

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the top has not changed (the chief executive since 2016 and the chair since 2016), but almost every layer below it has, in 18 months; the change signals an integration and cost reset run by a stable, centralised core, with the testing unit's leadership the weak point.

What changed: the CFO, Jouni Lintunen (since 2020), left on 6 February 2026 and was replaced by Ann Zetterberg, IAR Systems' CFO since 2021 (interim from January, permanent from 7 April 2026). The head of sales, Steffan Schumacher, left in March 2025 and Erik Rehn started in November 2025. US sales management was replaced in early 2026. At IAR, Karin Schreil replaced Cecilia Wachtmeister in May 2026. The Software Quality Solutions unit lost its head in October 2025 and its interim head in May 2026, and is now run by an interim leader outside the management team. A senior investor-relations lead is being hired. On the board, Jouni Toijala joined at the April 2026 AGM; Robert Ingman remains chair.

What it signals:

  • Appointing the acquired company's CFO as group CFO signals that IAR's integration and subscription conversion are the financial priority; it also brings someone who has run a perpetual-to-subscription transition before.
  • The US sales changes are a response to a known execution problem, not a strategic shift.
  • The testing unit, the business management wants to take from EUR 34.5 million (2024) to EUR 100 million and that recruitment is prioritised for, has had no permanent head for most of 2026. That is the clearest gap between stated priority and leadership resourcing.
  • The chief executive, Juha Varelius, holds 400,982 shares (about EUR 12 million), runs a centralised, two-person (CEO and CFO) decision model, and has no visible successor. Stability at the top is a strength for continuity and a key-person risk.

For the model, leadership change adds execution risk to the testing growth rate and the US recovery, not to the cost programme, which is already largely executed.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

C-6311th of 26

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the strategy has changed materially in emphasis, from "grow 20-30% a year" to "grow about 10% organically and earn about 30%", while the product strategy (a multi-product toolchain for the whole embedded development cycle) has deepened; the direction is well argued and funded, but poorly measured in public.

The changes:

  1. Ambition reset. The board's long-term target (set in 2023) was 20-30% annual growth; the chief executive now states a long-term organic growth aim of about 10% and a structural operating margin of about 30% from 2027. That is a pivot from growth-first to profit-first, made under pressure after two guidance cuts in 2025.
  2. A full-lifecycle toolchain. With IAR, QT now covers chip selection (certified compilers for more than 15,000 microcontrollers from 70 chipmakers), user interface design and development (Qt, Qt for MCUs, Clate), and testing and static analysis (Squish, Axivion, Coco). IAR's role is to bring QT into projects at the moment the chip is chosen, before the user interface is designed, and to sell testing into IAR's installed base.
  3. Monetisation. IAR moves from perpetual licences to subscriptions within three years; group pricing moves gradually from seats towards capacity, usage and compliance.
  4. Go-to-market. Sales capacity moves from Western automotive towards defence, medical and APAC; US sales are reorganised.
  5. Capital. Large M&A is paused; debt repayment comes first; bolt-ons later.

Articulation is clear in direction and candid in tone. Resourcing is adequate: the EUR 20 million of savings funds reinvestment in APAC sales and AI research, and R&D stays at 15-20% of sales. The weakness is measurement: no organic growth, no segment revenue (IAR, testing), no contract-duration split. The strategy can therefore be judged only through ARR, distribution licence revenue and margins, which is why investors keep a discount.

C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

C559th of 26

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: formal guidance has not been raised in 2026, although the business is well ahead of it; the informal ambition for 2027 (an operating margin of about 30%) acts as an unofficial target and is the most important guidance signal; the long-term growth ambition has been lowered, not raised.

Formal guidance: set on 26 February 2026 at constant-currency growth of at least 10% and an EBITA margin of at least 15%, about 43% below the consensus of the time. It was reiterated in May, August and October 2026 despite nine-month constant-currency growth of about 21%, reported growth of 19.1%, and an underlying EBITA margin of about 18%. On the nine-month figures, the revenue floor needs Q4 revenue of only about EUR 72 million (Q4 2025: EUR 77.1 million) and the margin floor needs Q4 EBITA of about EUR 16 million, against EUR 27.4 million in Q4 2025. Not raising guidance with this much headroom is a policy choice: management has stated it now publishes floors only, to avoid repeated downgrades.

Informal ambition: on the Q2 and Q3 2026 calls, the chief executive said 2027 will look substantially better, with an operating margin of about 30% (described elsewhere as 30%-plus) even under modest growth. This is not formal guidance, and in the same call management said the savings should not be read as a direct cut in next year's cost base because part will be reinvested. The two statements together mean that the 30% depends on revenue growth as well as savings.

Mid-term targets: none have been updated since the IAR acquisition; management declined to give consolidated mid-term targets. The testing business's EUR 100 million ambition and the long-term organic growth aim of about 10% are the only stated ambitions. The 2025-2027 share plan's thresholds (2027 revenue above EUR 333 million and an operating margin of at least 25%) were set before the downturn and are now well above consensus revenue (EUR 264 million).

For the model: guidance is not a constraint; the 2027 margin talk is the reference, and it is credible only if organic growth reaches high single digits.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

D5215th of 26

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: very little; the only exit is IAR's embedded security business (EUR 3 million of annual costs), while the perimeter as a whole has widened with IAR. Simplification is happening inside the organisation (merging administration and IT systems, legal entities and offices), not in the portfolio.

The security exit was the right call: duplicate development, little revenue, a cost saving of about 1% of group costs. The company also deliberately keeps consulting below 10% of revenue and avoids low-margin custom engineering. Beyond that, there are no low-return units to exit: the three product families (user interface, testing, compilers) share customers and sales channels. The remaining simplification is administrative: corporate entity mergers and office consolidations, which management says will bring further minor cost synergies.

For the model, perimeter change adds about EUR 3 million of savings (possibly already inside the EUR 20 million programme; the files do not say whether it is additional) and no revenue loss.

C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

C-6010th of 26

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the company is entering one large adjacent product market (certified microcontroller compilers, through IAR) and pushing harder into APAC, defence and low-cost devices; the moves are strategically sensible because they reuse the same customers, developers and chip partners, but the financial payback is slow and depends on cross-selling that is not yet visible in the numbers.

The moves:

  • Microcontroller toolchains (IAR). A USD 2-3 billion adjacent market growing about 10% a year (Business Foundations report). IAR's compilers are certified for functional safety and support more than 15,000 devices from 70 chipmakers, which gives QT a place at the moment the chip is selected and a sales lead for Qt for MCUs and testing.
  • Low-cost devices. Qt for MCUs on Infineon PSOC Edge, Nuvoton Gerda (November 2026: premium interfaces without external memory, which lowers device cost), and Qualcomm Dragonwing industrial edge-AI processors. This is where LVGL competes, so it is necessary defence as well as growth.
  • Verticals. Defence (FACE conformance), aerospace and medical, which are growing and less cyclical.
  • Geography. Hiring for APAC sales and AI research during the restructuring; strong traction with Chinese automotive makers.

Sensibleness: high on strategy, moderate on economics. IAR cost EUR 205 million for a business with revenue of about EUR 40 million that is now shrinking by design (consensus EUR 33 million in 2026). On FactSet segment consensus (EUR 47.4 million in 2028) and IAR's historical EBITDA margin of about 38%, IAR would earn roughly EUR 15-17 million a year by 2028, a pre-tax return of about 7-8% on the price before synergies. The case therefore needs cross-selling (testing into IAR's base, Qt for MCUs into chip-selection leads) and the subscription uplift to earn its cost of capital. APAC and defence expansion is cheap and funded from savings.

C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

B+643rd of 26

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms, and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent?

Judgement: price rises are real on developer licences (5-10% a year on list price, above wage inflation of about 3%, and applied at each renewal, now annual for many customers); they are not real on distribution royalties (fixed unit prices, no indexation, so falling in real terms); and the larger pricing change, from seats to capacity and usage, is latent and stated by management.

Evidence of real increases:

  • Annual list price increases of 5-10% on 1-year developer contracts (DNB Carnegie), and about 5% cited by management; the cut in the 3-year discount from 33% to 10% raised the effective 3-year price by about 35%. Commercial peers pass through 3-5% a year, so QT prices above the industry norm.
  • The move from 3-year to 1-year contracts means customers now face the annual increase every year rather than every three years; DNB Carnegie notes that a series of 1-year contracts yields more revenue over time than one 3-year contract.
  • IAR's conversion to subscription: the Industry digest records that conversions of this kind typically raise what the customer pays (Temenos term licences about 30% more; ATOSS licence-to-cloud 30-40%).

Evidence of the cost of pricing: a former account manager (2023) said large price rises cost QT a significant share of small and mid-sized customers; expert calls report developer frustration and early evaluations of Flutter and LVGL; customers respond by buying the minimum seat count. Price is therefore real but partly paid for in seat volume.

Distribution royalties: unit prices are fixed for the life of a design, with no inflation pass-through and no volume discounts granted; the real price falls with inflation, although QT has resisted cuts even in downturns.

Latent: capacity-based, usage-based and compliance-based pricing, to be tested with new customers in selected markets. Management cautioned analysts against modelling any revenue from it yet.

For the model: assume developer price/mix of about +5% a year, offset by seat volume of 0 to -3% while Western R&D is weak; royalty price flat in nominal terms.

C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

C5510th of 26

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: M&A stepped up sharply in 2025 (IAR, EUR 205 million, the largest deal in QT's history) and has now been paused; the strategic logic is strong and integration is going well operationally, but the financial discipline is only moderate, because the price is hard to justify without revenue synergies that are not yet evident.

Record: froglogic (2021) and Axivion (2022) were small and successful; testing revenue tripled to EUR 34.5 million by 2024 by selling them into Qt's developer base. IAR (completed 17 October 2025) was funded with cash and a EUR 150 million loan.

Integration: core functions were integrated within 12 months; the main technical milestones are complete, with some legal entity mergers and office consolidations still to come; the subscription conversion is ahead of plan (68% of IAR bookings in Q2 2026 against a 40% target, a majority again in Q3); the security business was closed; IAR's CFO became group CFO. Management reports no major administrative problems.

Discipline: transaction costs were EUR 5.8 million; acquired-intangible amortisation rose to about EUR 12.5 million a year, which cuts reported EPS by about EUR 0.40; return on capital employed fell from 43.4% (2024) to 15.5% (2025). IAR's standalone result is currently in the red because of the subscription shift. As shown under C-16, the return on the price is about 7-8% pre-tax by 2028 before synergies. The deal is defensible as buying a strategic position (chip-selection access, certified compilers, a SaaS conversion option) rather than earnings.

Outlook: management says future deals will be high-quality product companies, not purchased revenue, and that the near-term focus is integration. The model should assume no further acquisitions before 2028 and debt repayment instead.

C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

F4222nd of 26

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the balance sheet was used once, heavily, for IAR; it is now being repaired, with no dividend, no buyback and none mooted; shareholder returns are not a lever in the 18-month horizon, although cash flow will make them possible from 2028.

Facts: net debt moved from net cash of EUR 86.9 million (Q3 2025) to net debt of EUR 103 million at end-2025 and EUR 76.5 million at Q3 2026 (1.3x EBITDA on FactSet's trailing basis, 1.4x on Inderes'); EUR 15 million of the bank loan was repaid in H1 2026; the equity ratio is about 54%. The company has never paid a dividend; the AGM authorised the board to buy back up to 2 million shares (about 8%) and to issue up to 2 million, but no buyback has been made and Inderes expects none near term. Consensus total debt falls to EUR 80 million at end-2026, EUR 40 million at end-2027 and EUR 1 million at end-2028.

Interpretation: cash generation (consensus free cash flow EUR 40.8 million in 2026, EUR 53.8 million in 2027, EUR 62.8 million in 2028) will remove net debt by about end-2027 to mid-2028. After that, the question becomes whether QT buys back shares, pays a dividend or buys another company. The history (no dividends since the 2016 demerger, a growth-reinvestment culture, a chief executive who sees M&A as the main growth engine) points to M&A rather than returns. Not buying back shares at a 24-month-forward P/E of 13.4x, the 7th percentile of five years, is a missed opportunity, but a reasonable one while net debt is above 1x.

For the model: no dividends or buybacks in 2026-2028; debt repaid from free cash flow; net interest falling from about EUR 4.5 million in 2026 to near zero by 2028.

C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–65

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: four more positive forces are present: AI products launched on time and integrated across the toolchain, the hidden renewal benefit of shorter contracts, better disclosure, and partnerships with chipmakers that pull QT into designs earlier.

  1. AI products on time. Clate, an AI-native design tool that writes production-ready QML code, launched in beta at the Qt World Summit on 27 October 2026, with full release in early 2027; AI assistants run across Qt, Design Studio and Squish; Squish has an AI autonomous tester; Axivion checks NVIDIA CUDA code inside the NVIDIA Halos inspection lab. This lowers the skill barrier to using Qt, which widens the developer base and counters LVGL's ease of use with AI agents.
  2. Shorter contracts as a future tailwind. Because about 95% of contract value is recognised upfront, the 2025 shift from 3-year to 1-year deals cut recognised revenue. As those 1-year contracts renew each year at higher prices, recognised revenue catches up from 2026-2027, and contract assets and receivables fall, which helps cash conversion (free cash flow was 123% of net income in 2025).
  3. Disclosure and investor relations. Quarterly ARR and distribution revenue, an investor-relations lead, and a more cautious, more credible guidance style reduce the opacity discount that the Risks report blames for persistent short theses.
  4. Chip partnerships. Qualcomm (industrial AI), Infineon (PSOC Edge), Nuvoton (Gerda), NVIDIA (Halos, CUDA safety), plus IAR's 70 chip partners: pre-integration puts QT into reference designs, so design wins arrive before customers choose a user-interface tool.

Plausible: usage-based pricing for testing and compliance, a CRA-driven conversion wave in 2027, and a return to the 20%-plus margins of the testing business as AI code volumes rise.

C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

C-5211th of 26

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: there is no sign of pressure for change from the owners; the largest holder supports the current management and approach, there is no activist, and the register gives no catalyst in either direction.

The Ingman group holds 21.6% (June 2026) and its head, Robert Ingman, has chaired the board since 2016; Ingman entities bought small amounts of stock in May 2026 at about EUR 26.6 (1,995 shares in total, a token amount against a 5.5 million-share holding). The next largest holder is Ilmarinen (5.4%); Goldman Sachs crossed 5% again in June 2026, partly through derivatives. There is no activist, no published short report, and short positions are below the Nordic reporting thresholds. Nine flagging notices in H1 2026 show active institutional trading, not pressure.

Implications: (1) management has its main owner's backing for the profit-first reset, the IAR conversion and the zero-dividend policy; (2) a takeover would need the Ingman group's support, so the buyer's floor discussed in C-09 is real but gated; (3) the absence of external pressure means no one is pushing for buybacks or segment disclosure except the brokers (DNB Carnegie has asked for contract-duration and segment disclosure).

C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

C+636th of 26

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the positive corporate changes are strong in cost and portfolio discipline, moderately broad (cost, IAR conversion, pricing, products, disclosure), and well funded by cash flow; they are narrow where it matters most for valuation, the organic demand engine and the US, and they rest on a thinly staffed layer below the chief executive.

The scale of the self-help is large relative to the company: EUR 20 million of savings is about 9% of 2025 revenue and about 40% of 2025 EBITA; the end of EUR 9.7 million of restructuring costs and of IAR's revenue trough add further. Together they explain most of the consensus EBITA rise from about EUR 42 million (2026, reported) to about EUR 79 million (2027). These changes are within management's control and largely executed, which is why the 2027 recovery is more predictable than QT's history would suggest.

What is weaker: the changes do not by themselves restore organic growth. The US reorganisation, the CRA-driven conversions, the testing push and the new pricing models are all at an early stage, with interim leadership in testing and no public metrics to track them. Resourcing is adequate in money (R&D at 15-20% of sales, APAC hiring) but stretched in people after an 18% reduction.

For the model: treat the cost and IAR changes as high-confidence drivers of the 2027 margin, with the 30% EBITA margin as an upper-central case (we would centre on 27-30%, depending on organic growth), and treat the demand-side changes as optionality for 2028 and beyond.

C-22

What staircase of novel positive developments lies ahead: which new, dated things can the market look forward to within the next 6 months, in months 6 to 18, and in months 18 to 36; how novel is each; and how well-founded?

A641st of 26

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the staircase is well populated in the next 18 months and almost all of it is mechanical rather than speculative: the end of restructuring, the first quarters in which IAR is in both periods, IAR's return to growth, a reported margin that roughly doubles, and debt approaching zero. The most novel steps (capacity-based pricing, a Cyber Resilience Act conversion wave, testing disclosure, a first-ever capital return) are further out and less certain.

Next 6 months (to May 2027):

  1. Q4 2026 results, 18 February 2027: 2026 floors comfortably beaten (on the nine-month run-rate), Q4 expected to be the year's strongest EBITA, no further major restructuring charges, first floors for 2027. Novelty moderate; foundation high.
  2. Clate's full release (early 2027) and further AI features. Novelty moderate for the market (the first AI-native product from QT), foundation high on timing, low on revenue.
  3. Q1 2027 results (about late April or May 2027): the first quarter with IAR in both years, so reported growth becomes close to organic growth (plus IAR's own recovery) for the first time; against a weak Q1 2026 (EBITA margin 9.6%), the reported margin should jump. Novelty high for perception, because the "IAR masks the organic trend" argument loses force; foundation high on mechanics, moderate on the organic number.
  4. The EU Product Liability Directive transposition (9 December 2026) and CRA reporting already live: evidence of open-source-to-commercial conversions in management commentary. Novelty moderate; foundation moderate.

Months 6 to 18 (May 2027 to May 2028):

  1. The 2027 margin step-up: quarterly EBITA margins of roughly 25-30% against 11-15% reported a year earlier; EPS up about 80% for the year on consensus. Novelty high in optics, low in information (it is expected); foundation high on costs, moderate on size.
  2. IAR back to growth (2027) with a majority of customers on subscription; a possible first disclosure of IAR or ARR by unit. Foundation high on growth, low on disclosure.
  3. Net debt near zero (end-2027 to mid-2028): the first time since the IAR deal that capital allocation becomes a live question (buyback, first dividend or M&A). Novelty high if returns; foundation low (the history points to M&A).
  4. The CRA's full application (December 2027), likely pulling compliance purchases forward into 2027. Foundation moderate.
  5. A capital markets day with new mid-term targets after the integration and reset. Not announced; plausible; novelty high.

Months 18 to 36 (2028-2029):

  1. Capacity, usage and compliance-based pricing moving from pilots to the installed base, the first pricing line not tied to seats, which the Sector digest says can reprice a cohort. Novelty high; foundation low to moderate.
  2. Testing approaching EUR 60-70 million on its 20% growth path, and possibly separate disclosure. Novelty moderate; foundation moderate.
  3. Royalties from the 2022-2025 design wins (Chinese electric cars, the Suzuki e Vitara, consumer-electronics wins) reaching volume if production recovers. Foundation moderate; depends on the cycle.
  4. RISC-V and edge-AI microcontroller adoption widening IAR's market. Foundation moderate.

The narrative's weak step is the gap between steps 5 and 10: once the margin restoration is reported in 2027, the market will need evidence of organic growth near 10% and of the pricing change to extend the staircase.
