Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

A651st of 26

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

A651st of 26

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

QT sits at the most profitable point of a supportive value chain, with benign commercial rivals, fragmented customers and partner-like suppliers; the structure has passed a real stress test (2025) without any loss of price or share. Its fragility is not competitive but structural-technological: the seat as the unit of price, and the low end of the market where free tools and AI meet. The evidence from 2025 is the strongest point in the case: when QT was weakest, no rival attacked, prices held and customers stayed; the downturn showed only in seats, contract length and royalty volumes. That makes the industry structure a durable support for margins and pricing over the model horizon. The risks that the structure does not protect against are (1) fewer seats per customer as AI raises engineering productivity, and (2) slower share gains in cheap microcontroller devices, where QT's royalty growth must come from. For the modeller, the industry supports pricing assumptions of about +5% a year on developer licences and flat royalty rates, stable share in the core, and a margin structure that can return to 30%-plus; it does not support assuming rapid share gains at the low end.

3a

Value chain dynamics

6 answers
C-3258C-3366C-3466C-3764C-3856C-3962
C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?D5817th of 26full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

B+663rd of 26

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the route to market helps: QT sells directly and owns the customer and developer relationship, intermediaries have negligible power, and chipmakers act as an unpaid lead-generation channel. The weak point is internal (US sales execution), not intermediaries.

Structure: direct sales teams with local pre-sales engineers in EMEA, the Americas and APAC (hubs such as Detroit and Tokyo for automotive), selling to engineering teams and increasingly to middle management for testing tools; distributors and resellers serve only small regional accounts and have no pricing power. The open-source edition of Qt acts as a top-of-funnel: developers use it free and QT converts them to commercial licences (the CRA strengthens the conversion argument). Chipmakers (Qualcomm, Infineon, NXP, Renesas, STMicroelectronics, Nuvoton, NVIDIA) pre-integrate Qt and IAR tools into reference designs, so QT reaches projects before a customer chooses tools, at no acquisition cost.

Hindrances: (1) the US sales organisation underperformed through 2025-2026 and was restructured; (2) testing tools are sold to middle management with sales cycles up to 18 months, which slows the cross-sell; (3) the open-source funnel works only if commercial licence enforcement does not alienate the community, and aggressive compliance-based selling has created friction among mid-tier developers (Risks report).

Fragility: low. No intermediary can disintermediate QT; the main risk is that developers' first choice for cheap devices shifts to a free library, which would weaken the funnel slowly.

C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

A662nd of 26

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: existing competitors are behaving in a way that helps QT: they are retreating (Flutter embedded), failing to enter (Unity), fading (Kanzi, EB GUIDE) or staying in their niche (Altia, Crank, Android Automotive in infotainment). QT's position against existing commercial competitors is robust; its exposure is to free alternatives, not to these companies.

Detail: Google's Flutter has reportedly moved embedded work into maintenance mode after engineering cuts; Android Automotive dominates centre-console infotainment but lacks functional-safety compliance for instrument clusters, and carmakers that want data control and lightweight performance choose Qt for cockpits; Unity's automotive push produced no impact or price pressure; Kanzi and EB GUIDE suffer from a lack of developers; HTML5 and Electron are too heavy for embedded hardware. In testing, QT out-scales fragmented specialists by selling through its global sales network. In compilers, IAR's certified toolchain competes with Arm, Green Hills, Tasking and SEGGER in a stable market.

Where an existing competitor could hurt: Android Automotive expanding from infotainment into safety-relevant displays if Google obtains safety certification (Business Foundations "other side of the story"), and Altia or Crank winning low-end microcontroller projects with more flexible licensing.

Fragility: low against named commercial competitors over the 18-month to four-year horizon; the Wisdom Library's distinction applies: QT's barriers block entrants (certifications, ecosystem) and its advantages beat rivals (talent pool, toolchain breadth), and both are intact.

C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?B+645th of 26full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

C5610th of 26

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: disruption acts most strongly on QT's customers' engineering teams (AI raises output per engineer) and on the low end of competition (AI makes free tools easier), and acts in QT's favour on regulators (more certification) and on chip suppliers (more capable, cheaper chips that can run rich interfaces). The company's position is moderately robust; the most fragile point is the number of developer seats.

By actor:

  • Customers (demand). AI coding tools let engineering teams do more with fewer people (seat compression of perhaps 5-10% at large accounts, per DNB Carnegie's illustration) while generating more code to test (more demand for Squish, Axivion, Coco). Carmakers shifting to software-defined vehicles and centralised compute raise software content per car.
  • Competitors. AI lowers the cost of building user-interface tools and libraries and makes free libraries easier to use; it also lets QT ship AI features (Clate) that make its own tools easier.
  • Regulators and political actors. More mandatory security and safety documentation, which AI-generated code cannot satisfy alone.
  • Suppliers (chipmakers). RISC-V adoption and cheap edge-AI chips widen the market for certified compilers (IAR) and rich interfaces on microcontrollers.
  • Customers' supply chain. Tariffs and regionalisation disrupt production schedules, which hits royalties.

Fragility: the forces leave QT's price per seat, its certification position and its testing demand stronger, and its seat volume and low-end share weaker. In model terms: developer seat volume carries a negative bias of 1-3% a year from 2027 if pricing does not move to capacity, offset by testing growth of 15-20% and compliance-driven conversions.

Industry structure

Judgement65C-2472C-2568C-2664C-2766C-2868C-2962C-3166C-3555C-3652
Group judgementGroup judgement: Industry and Value Chain (Layer 1)A651st of 26full answer under 3 Value chain dynamics and industry structure
C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

B724th of 26

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: QT is the number one commercial vendor of cross-platform user-interface frameworks for embedded devices, by a wide margin; in testing tools it is a mid-sized consolidator in a fragmented market; with IAR it is one of the two or three leading independent commercial microcontroller compiler vendors. In the wider embedded-software tools market (USD 5-10 billion) it holds only about 3-5%.

User-interface frameworks: Qt has more than 1.5 million developers, more than 3,500 paying corporate customers and a single code base from microcontrollers to high-end cockpits. Hyundai and Kia use it across all vehicle platforms; Ford, Mercedes-Benz, Suzuki (e Vitara cockpit), LG and others are customers; penetration in North American infotainment and instrument clusters is put at 15-20% of shipped vehicles. Commercial rivals (Altia, Crank, Rightware's Kanzi, Elektrobit's EB GUIDE) are a fraction of QT's size and are losing momentum. One expert's claim of "over 90% adoption" in automotive user interfaces is not credible as a market share and should not be modelled; the defensible statement is that QT is the default commercial choice and the main alternatives are not commercial vendors at all (open source, Android Automotive for infotainment, in-house code).

Testing and quality: about EUR 40 million of revenue in 2026 (DNB Carnegie) in a fragmented market of specialist vendors and service providers; QT's edge is that it sells development and testing together.

Compilers: IAR's Embedded Workbench supports more than 15,000 microcontrollers from 70 chipmakers and holds functional-safety certifications; its rivals are Arm's Keil, Green Hills, Tasking, SEGGER and free toolchains (GCC, LLVM) supplied by chipmakers.

The distance to number one matters only in the broader toolchain market, where QT is small next to Siemens, Synopsys or Cadence in electronic design; QT is not trying to be number one there. In its own niche it is already number one.

C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

B+684th of 26

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: concentrated in QT's core: the top five commercial vendors hold more than 50% of the premium commercial embedded-tools market, and the commercial embedded GUI segment has a steep, leader-plus-tail shape; the tail is fragmented, and the real competition is free software rather than commercial rivals.

The Business Foundations report states that the top five commercial vendors control more than 50% of the premium commercial embedded-tools market, that leaders are buying niche static-analysis and GUI-testing vendors, and that a scale threshold of about USD 100 million of ARR is needed to fund a global sales force and safety certification. QT, at EUR 160 million of ARR, is above that threshold and its GUI rivals are far below it. So the top five clearly hold more than 30%, and the share curve is steep: QT is several times larger than the next commercial GUI vendor, which places the top one alone well above the five-to-one ratio the question asks about.

Two qualifications: (1) market-share data for this niche is poor; the evidence is size and customer lists, not audited shares; (2) the market definition matters, because the binding alternative for many projects, especially cheap consumer devices, is a free library (LVGL), the free open-source version of Qt itself, or in-house code. Concentration among paid vendors therefore overstates pricing power at the low end, while it understates QT's position in safety-certified automotive, medical and industrial projects, where free alternatives lack certification and support.

C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

B645th of 26

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: commercial shares are stable and QT's has risen; new commercial entrants have taken almost nothing; the share that has moved is developer mindshare at the low end, towards open-source libraries, and that shift is unmeasured.

Evidence of stability: QT has steadily expanded its share of the commercial embedded GUI market over three years (Business Foundations report) and has become the default for software-defined vehicle cockpits; Unity's entry into automotive user interfaces failed to cause share loss or price pressure (management, Q2 2025); Kanzi and EB GUIDE are fading because of a shortage of specialist developers (Hyundai executive); Flutter's embedded push stalled. Customer churn is low and customers rarely switch once a product is built on Qt, because changing the framework means rewriting the user interface and retraining engineers.

What moved: LVGL and Slint have gained users in cost-sensitive microcontroller devices, a segment QT is now targeting (Qt for MCUs, the IAR link). A former head of QT's microcontroller tools said in 2023 that these younger platforms challenge the legacy code base and are capturing share in consumer internet-of-things devices. No figures exist. Separately, QT lost some small and mid-sized customers after price increases (expert account, 2023).

For the model: assume stable share in the core (automotive clusters, medical, industrial, defence) and treat the low end as a growth market in which QT's share is uncertain; QT's Qt for MCUs growth will be the evidence.

C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

C+665th of 26

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest and most advantaged competitors, and is this company among them?

Judgement: yes, the industry is consolidating from point tools into integrated toolchains, and QT is one of the consolidators and one of the most advantaged; the risk is that the very largest software and industrial groups are consolidating faster and could become either competitors or buyers.

The trend: buyers prefer fewer vendors and integrated design-develop-test workflows; leading players buy niche static-analysis and testing tools; across software, scale buyers are paying high prices (Schneider for PTC, Siemens for Altair, Synopsys for Ansys; earlier, Renesas bought Altium). QT has followed the same path at its own scale: froglogic (2021), Axivion (2022), IAR (2025). Its advantages compound: the largest developer community (which is also why AI models write Qt code well), safety certifications, silicon pre-integrations, and a direct sales force across three regions.

QT is among the most advantaged in its niche, and the consolidation logic favours it in two ways: customers standardise on platforms with talent pools (Hyundai's point about the shortage of Kanzi and Elektrobit developers), and fragmented testing vendors can be acquired or out-sold. The caution is that QT is a EUR 0.8 billion company in a consolidation wave led by companies a hundred times larger. Its position is that of a scarce, independent asset, which supports the takeover floor (C-09) but means it cannot win a spending war with a platform owner that decides to bundle a user-interface and testing toolchain.

C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

B686th of 26

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading What is competitors' attitude to competition: margin first or volume first; benign or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement: the commercial competitors are small and margin-conscious, and conduct is benign: prices rise 3-5% a year across the industry, QT raises them 5-10%, and prices did not fall in the 2025 volume downturn; customers cut seats and contract length instead. The "volume-first, loss-tolerant" actors are not commercial rivals but free software and big-technology platforms.

Evidence: commercial leaders routinely pass through 3-5% annual licence increases and price-cutting is absent across enterprise accounts because switching costs and qualification cycles are high (Business Foundations report). QT kept raising developer prices through 2025 and refused to cut distribution royalties or grant volume discounts despite pressure from carmakers. Niche rivals (Altia, Kanzi) are cheaper but lack a full toolchain and compete on single projects.

What happens when volume falls: in 2025, price held and volume adjusted. Customers bought the minimum seat count, shifted from 3-year to 1-year contracts and delayed large deals; average deal size fell while deal count held. That is the behaviour of a market with pricing discipline: the downturn shows in seats and timing, not in price.

The loss-tolerant players: Google (Flutter, Android Automotive) pursues developer reach rather than tool profit and has repeatedly reduced its commitment; open-source projects (LVGL, Slint) price at zero by design. Their attitude is not to win a price war but to make the commercial product unnecessary for some projects.

C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

B626th of 26

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years? Would competitors launch a "shark attack" on pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement: the commercial rivals' attitude is stable and they lack the scale to attack; there has been no change in conduct through QT's 2025 weakness, which was the obvious moment for an attack. The unstable actors are the large platforms and open-source communities, whose behaviour depends on strategy rather than on QT's weakness.

The 2025 test: QT cut guidance twice, its shares fell 51%, its sales organisation was disrupted, and yet both brokers report no change in the competitive environment and management saw no price pressure from Unity or others. No commercial rival used the moment to undercut. This is the strongest evidence that the commercial structure is stable.

Where instability can come from:

  • Tradera-style free offers. In the Vend peer file, Tradera launched free listings for car dealers to exploit Vend's platform disruption; the analogue for QT would be a rival or platform offering free tools during QT's IAR transition, but there is no sign of it.
  • Google. Its developer-tool commitments change with its own priorities (CTOs cite fears of Google abandoning frameworks, which also limits Flutter's adoption in long-life products).
  • Carmaker consortia. The Q2 Earnings Review flags the long-term risk of a royalty-free, open-source GUI framework backed by carmakers trying to cut licence costs. That would be a deliberate attack on royalty pricing, but none is under way.
  • AI-native tools. New design-to-code tools could price aggressively to win developers; they are early.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

B665th of 26

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: the structure is strongly supportive: QT sits at the most profitable stage of the value chain (software tools with gross margins above 80% and EBIT margins of 25-35%, against car-industry suppliers at about 6%), faces small, rational commercial rivals and sells to fragmented customers with high switching costs. The support is robust in safety-critical and complex projects and fragile at the low end of the market and in the per-seat pricing unit.

Sources of support: (1) high switching costs (rewriting user interfaces, retraining developers, re-certifying safety-relevant software); (2) certification barriers (ISO 26262 ASIL D, IEC 61508 SIL 4) that free tools lack; (3) chipmaker pre-integration, which makes QT part of the reference design; (4) no customer above 10% of revenue; (5) benign pricing conduct.

Fragilities, in order of importance:

  1. The seat as the unit of price. AI productivity can reduce seats without any competitor doing anything; the structure protects QT's price per seat, not the number of seats.
  2. The low end. In cheap consumer and internet-of-things devices, free libraries are good enough and increasingly easy to use with AI coding agents; this is the market QT needs for royalty growth.
  3. Royalty volumes. The structure protects royalty rates but not customers' production volumes.

The structure is less fragile than the Risks report's 35-40% failure probability implies; that figure combines cyclical, currency and structural risks, and the structural part alone is a slow erosion risk rather than a sudden one.

C-35To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?C-5510th of 26full answer under 1a External headwinds/threats
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

D5215th of 26

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: substitutes are a moderate threat that is slowly rising at the low end and falling in regulated segments; AI changes the economics of the main substitutes (writing it yourself, or using a free library) faster than it changes the economics of buying Qt.

The substitutes:

  • In-house code (raw C/C++). Historically too slow and costly; AI coding agents lower that cost, especially for simple screens.
  • Free libraries (LVGL, Slint) and the open-source Qt edition. Good enough for cheap devices; LVGL's lightness makes it easy for coding agents.
  • Web technologies. Too heavy for embedded hardware; not a growing threat.
  • Android Automotive for infotainment. Already established; not spreading into safety-relevant displays.
  • For testing: manual testing (shrinking) and AI-based test generation (emerging).
  • For compilers: free GCC and LLVM toolchains supplied by chipmakers.

How it is changing: two opposite forces. AI makes the substitutes cheaper for simple, unregulated devices. Regulation (CRA, product liability, functional safety) makes the substitutes more expensive for anything connected or safety-relevant, because a free library does not come with a declaration of conformity, long-term support or certified tools. The net is a widening split: QT's position strengthens in regulated, complex products (most of its revenue) and weakens in simple consumer devices (a small share of revenue but an important royalty growth pool).

4a

Business quality

A682nd of 268 answers
Judgement68C-4172C-4266C-4366C-4468C-4568C-4645C-4764
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

A682nd of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

QT's competitive advantage is strong, applied and mostly widening in the products that make most of its revenue: complex, long-life, safety-relevant devices where Qt is a critical purchase protected by switching costs, a developer standard and certification. The proof is behavioural rather than claimed: QT raised prices through its worst year without losing customers, rivals did not attack, and named carmakers keep choosing it. Two weaknesses limit the score: QT captures rather than shares value, which has cost it small customers and developer goodwill and leaves the cheap-device segment open to free alternatives; and its advantage protects the price per seat but not the number of seats, which AI may reduce. For the modeller: the advantage justifies assuming continued real price increases (about +5% a year on developer licences), a stable share in the core, and margins that can return to and exceed the 2024 level; it argues for modest assumptions on low-end share gains and for a gradual drag on seat volume from 2027 until capacity-based pricing is proven.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

A722nd of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: QT has three strong moats (switching costs embedded in customers' code and certification, a standardisation network effect in the developer community, and regulatory certification) and two supporting ones (silicon pre-integration and toolchain breadth). Certification and toolchain breadth are widening; the developer community's goodwill and, over the long term, the code-rewrite switching cost are at risk of narrowing because of pricing friction and AI.

  1. Switching costs (customer-related, strong, stable). The types, in the Wisdom Library's terms: procedural and learning costs (developers trained in QML and Qt C++), financial and time costs (rewriting the user interface of a multi-year product), and risk costs (re-certifying safety-relevant software). For safety products the purchase is "critical" rather than "necessary" in Towson's classification: failure is expensive, qualifying an alternative takes years, and the licence is a small part of the product's cost, which is the strongest pricing position. Evidence: low churn; customers leave only when products move into maintenance.
  2. Standardisation network effect (culture and knowledge, strong, widening). About 1.5 million developers create a talent pool that makes Qt the default (Hyundai: Kanzi and EB GUIDE are limited by the lack of developers). Qt's large presence in AI training data extends this: AI agents write Qt code well, which keeps Qt the platform of choice for AI-assisted teams.
  3. Certification (regulatory, strong, widening). TÜV certification up to ISO 26262 ASIL D and IEC 61508 SIL 4 (Coco), IAR's certified compilers, Qt 6.12's CRA declaration of conformity. Each new safety and security rule raises the barrier against free tools.
  4. Silicon pre-integration (production-related, moderate, widening). Partnerships with Qualcomm, Infineon, NXP, Renesas, STMicroelectronics, NVIDIA and, through IAR, 70 chipmakers place QT in reference designs before tool selection.
  5. Toolchain breadth (scale, moderate, widening). Design, development, testing and compilation from one vendor, which no GUI rival offers.

Narrowing: (a) developer goodwill, damaged by aggressive commercial enforcement and price rises; (b) a long-term risk that AI coding agents make porting a user interface to another framework much cheaper, which would erode the rewrite cost that underpins moat 1. The rewrite cost does not erode for certified products, so the erosion risk is concentrated in unregulated devices.

C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

B665th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: QT is large relative to its niche rivals and tiny relative to the platform companies; its scale is enough to fund certification, a global direct sales force and partnerships with every major chipmaker, which niche rivals cannot match, but not enough to win a spending contest with Google, Siemens or Synopsys.

QT: revenue of about EUR 243 million in 2026, ARR of EUR 160 million (Q2 2026), about 1,000 employees, of whom about a third work in product development (317 on average in 2025), and R&D of 15-20% of sales (about EUR 40-50 million a year). Niche GUI rivals (Altia, Crank, Rightware, Elektrobit's GUI unit) are far smaller, and the Business Foundations report puts the scale threshold for a global sales and certification organisation at about USD 100 million of ARR, which only QT exceeds in its niche. The platform companies are 50-500 times larger (Planisware's file notes Atlassian spends USD 3.3 billion a year on R&D).

What scale confers: (1) the developer pool and AI training-data presence (C-41); (2) the ability to support hundreds of hardware platforms and operating systems and to keep pre-integrations current; (3) a direct sales force in three regions with local engineers; (4) the cost of certification spread over many customers; (5) negotiating standing with chipmakers. What it does not confer: purchasing power (QT buys little) or the ability to fund an AI platform from scratch. QT's AI strategy therefore has to ride on others' models (it adds agents to its own tools) rather than compete with them.

C-43How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?C6611th of 26full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

B+684th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: inferiority is disproved for complex, cross-platform and safety-relevant products, where QT leads; for simple, cost-sensitive devices QT is not demonstrably superior to free alternatives, and customers' price sensitivity there is high.

Evidence that disproves inferiority in the core:

  • Customers keep paying above-market price rises and stay: low churn, renewals on 1-year terms with annual increases.
  • Named customers choose Qt over alternatives: Hyundai and Kia across every platform (citing reliable long-term updates and support), Ford continuing to prioritise Qt over rising open-source alternatives (2024), Suzuki choosing Qt for its mainstream electric cockpit, carmakers choosing Qt over Android Automotive for clusters to keep data control.
  • Productivity: Qt for MCUs is cited at 25-30% faster user-interface development than open-source alternatives (Business Foundations report); one code base from microcontroller to high-end cockpit; certified tools that shorten safety qualification.
  • Competitors lose on talent: specialist rivals cannot find developers.

Where utility is weaker: experts describe polarised developer opinions of the legacy code base, a request for better 3D support and web integration (Hyundai, 2022), a non-trivial runtime footprint on the cheapest microcontrollers (where LVGL is lighter), and licence costs that are a barrier for start-ups. Management itself says its tools are "not the cheapest" and that buyers pay to avoid costly recalls.

The verdict for the investment case: QT passes the "not inferior" test where most of its revenue sits; at the low end, its surplus utility depends on Qt for MCUs, Clate and AI assistants closing the ease-of-use gap.

C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

A683rd of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: QT has and uses pricing power, demonstrably: 5-10% annual list increases on developer licences, a cut in multi-year discounts from 33% to 10%, and a refusal to discount royalties, all held through a severe downturn. The erosion of surplus utility is real but limited to small customers, developer goodwill and seat counts, not to churn of established customers.

Applied evidence:

  • Annual list price rises of 5-10% on 1-year developer contracts (DNB Carnegie), against industry norms of 3-5%.
  • The 3-year renewal discount reduced from 33% to 10%, an effective increase of about 35% for customers renewing on 3-year terms.
  • The 2020 move from perpetual to subscription licences (about 80% of the active base by end-2022) and the move of revenue recognition from 70% to 95% upfront.
  • Royalty unit prices held firm and no volume discounts granted, despite carmaker pressure in 2025.
  • Prices were raised during 2025, the weakest year in a decade, without a rise in churn.

Erosion: a former account manager reported the loss of a significant part of the small and mid-sized customer base after large price rises (2023); developers have evaluated Flutter and LVGL because of pricing; customers responded to 2025's price rises by buying fewer seats and shorter terms. The Leadership report places QT's pricing in the most aggressive quartile among cross-platform framework vendors.

Assessment: the power is strongest where the purchase is critical (safety-relevant, long-life products) and weakest in simple devices. The next test is whether capacity-based pricing can raise revenue per customer as AI raises output per developer; that would be pricing power applied to a new unit and is not yet evidenced.

C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

C-4510th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto?

Judgement: very little; QT's model is to capture rather than share the value its scale creates, through above-inflation price rises and no volume discounts. It shares value only indirectly, through the free open-source edition and through productivity gains in its tools. This works while switching costs are high, but it leaves the low end open to cheaper alternatives and makes the move to AI-era pricing harder.

Evidence: annual price rises above inflation; no volume discounts on royalties; commercial licence enforcement against open-source users; the effective 35% increase from the cut in multi-year discounts. The only sharing is the open-source edition (which builds the developer funnel), the inclusion of new AI features and tools in subscriptions, and productivity: faster development with Qt for MCUs and Clate reduces customers' engineering costs, so value is shared through time-to-market rather than price.

The Wisdom Library's "scale economies shared" pattern (low prices that make the moat self-reinforcing) does not describe QT. The relevance for the model: QT's margins should be high and its price assumptions positive, but the absence of sharing is one reason customers minimise seats, why small customers churned and why free alternatives win simple projects. If QT moves to capacity-based pricing, the design of that pricing (sharing part of the AI productivity gain or capturing it all) will decide whether customers accept it.

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

B646th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: several mechanical forces work for QT each year: royalties from past design wins that reach production with a three-to-five-year lag, a growing installed base of products built on Qt, annual price escalators on a recurring base, ratcheting safety and security regulation, and rising code volume to test. They are offset by two mechanical headwinds: AI-driven seat compression and the real-terms erosion of fixed royalty prices. On balance the gravity is positive but moderate, and less automatic than the Business Foundations report suggests, because royalties depend on customers' production volumes.

Positive mechanics:

  1. Royalty pipeline. Design wins of 2022-2025 (for example Chinese electric cars, Suzuki e Vitara, consumer-electronics wins) generate royalties as products ship, without further sales cost. Management cites the growing number of displays as a structural driver.
  2. Installed-base lock-in. Every product built on Qt raises future switching costs and maintenance and renewal revenue.
  3. Price escalators. Annual 5-10% list increases on developer licences, now applied annually to more customers because contracts are shorter.
  4. Regulation. Each new rule (CRA, product liability, safety standards) adds required documentation and testing.
  5. Code volume. AI-generated code raises the testing workload regardless of QT's actions.
  6. IAR conversion. Subscription revenue builds a recurring base that grows mechanically once the transition ends (2027).

Negative mechanics: fewer developers per project as AI tools spread; fixed royalty unit prices that lose about 2-3% a year in real terms; the dependence of royalties on production volumes (management expects royalties flat to slightly down over the medium term, which shows the pipeline is not yet offsetting the volume cycle).

Against peers: Planisware and similar engineering-software vendors have the price escalators and installed-base mechanics but not the royalty pipeline; Innoscripta has regulation but not lock-in. QT's mix is favourable but cyclical.

4b

Management quality

C599th of 2612 answers

Group judgement

Company - Management

C599th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Management is a long-tenured, owner-aligned, candid and strategically capable team led by a deal-oriented chief executive with an excellent long-term record, which has acted quickly and well since the 2025 shock; its weaknesses are forecasting optimism, a thin and unstable layer below the top two people, and an allocation style that favours acquisitions over returning cash. The governance and pay structures are clean and aligned; the strategy (a certified toolchain from chip to test, AI in the tools, pricing off the seat) is right; the record of acting in the last twelve months is strong. What limits the score is evidence of repeated over-optimism in growth planning (2022, 2024, 2025, and a share plan set at EUR 333 million for 2027), slow sales execution in the US and leadership turnover in the units that matter for growth. For the modeller: give high weight to management's cost and integration commitments, discount its growth ambitions by a few points, and expect M&A rather than buybacks once debt is repaid.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

C+666th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: the chief executive's long-term vision is big ("well beyond doubling or tripling" revenue; a full toolchain from chip to test; a EUR 100 million testing business), bigger than that of most European software peers of QT's size; the near-term ambition has been deliberately reset to about 10% organic growth and a 30% margin, which is credible but less bold.

Evidence: in February 2025 Varelius said he sees the company "going well beyond doubling or tripling this revenue on the longer term"; in 2024 he spoke of investing for growth "for five and ten years going forward"; the board's 2023 target was 20-30% annual growth. The multi-product strategy and the IAR deal (QT's largest) show the ambition in action, as does the push into AI tools and pricing models. The 2026 reset, a long-term organic growth aim of about 10%, floor-only guidance and a profit focus, is a response to the 2025 misses.

Against peers: Planisware aims to be the number one multi-specialty PPM provider and Infineon to lead AI power; QT's equivalent is to own the embedded development toolchain. QT's ambition is comparable in direction and bolder in M&A than Planisware, and more cautious now in growth targets than its own history.

The ambition is a force for the case as long as it is matched by delivery; the 2022 and 2025 misses show that ambition has previously outrun forecasting.

C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

C+666th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities?

Judgement: the strategy is smart and widens the moat: own the developer's workflow from chip selection to testing, sell certified tools into regulated markets, use chipmakers as a distribution channel, and move pricing off the seat before AI shrinks seat counts. Its main weaknesses are dependence on cyclical end-markets, a costly entry into compilers and a reluctance to disclose the evidence.

Right priorities: (1) testing and quality, the fastest-growing, least cyclical line, which AI strengthens; (2) the microcontroller market through IAR, where devices are growing fastest; (3) certification and compliance (CRA, functional safety) as a commercial weapon; (4) AI built into the tools (Clate, assistants) rather than ignored; (5) a gradual move to capacity and usage pricing, which is exactly what the Sector digest says seat sellers must do.

Bold: buying IAR at about five times revenue and immediately converting it to subscriptions, accepting a revenue fall; cutting 18% of staff while launching new products.

Dynamic across environments: the strategy works in a recovery (operating leverage, royalties), in a downturn (testing and regulated verticals hold up, costs have been cut) and under AI (testing demand, certified code), but it is exposed in a prolonged Western industrial recession, because all product lines depend on customers' R&D budgets.

What it lacks: diversification away from manufacturing customers, a clear answer for the cheapest devices beyond Qt for MCUs, and the transparency (organic growth, segment data) that would let investors value it.

C-57

How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

C-6411th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: a strong, decisive capital-markets operator with an exceptional long-term record (buying Qt from Nokia in 2012, demerging it in 2016 and scaling it about fivefold) and two clear weaknesses shown by actions: optimistic forecasting that produced misses in 2022 and 2025, and a slow first reaction to the 2025 downturn. He is an enabler, not an ossifier, but not an outlier operator.

Actions for: at Digia he bought the Qt business from Nokia in 2012, shifted Digia from IT services to software and demerged Qt in 2016 (Digia's shares rose almost 150% in 2015). At Qt he completed the move from perpetual to subscription licences (about 80% of the base by 2022), bought and scaled froglogic and Axivion (testing revenue tripled), grew revenue from EUR 58 million (2019) to EUR 209 million (2024) with margins rising to 34%, bought IAR and integrated it within a year, and launched a large reorganisation within six months of the profit warning. His pay fell as results fell (2025 entirely fixed), and he holds 400,982 shares.

Actions against: two 2025 guidance cuts after missing internal forecasts for three quarters; in the Q2 2025 call he dismissed cost cutting ("does not even think about it") before cutting heavily a few months later; the revenue thresholds of the 2022-2024 share plan (EUR 210 million or 240 million for 2024; the files report both) and of the 2025-2027 plan (EUR 333 million for 2027) show repeated over-optimism; the US sales problem persisted for more than a year; decision-making is centralised and he has no visible successor; he has a finance rather than a technical background.

His communication is unusually blunt and honest ("not good", "where we have gone wrong"), which helps trust. The pattern in the Wisdom Library is closer to a capital-allocating strategist than to the "animal" operator archetype.

C-57b

How high a priority is hiring well for the chief executive, and how great is the leadership team the chief executive has built (an enabler)?

F4623rd of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How high a priority is hiring well for the chief executive, and how great is the leadership team he has built?

Judgement: hiring is not visibly a strength: the team below the chief executive has turned over heavily, two key units (testing and IAR) have had leadership changes in 2026, decision-making rests on two people, and there is no visible succession. The long-tenured R&D leadership is the exception.

Evidence: in 18 months the company lost its CFO (replaced by IAR's CFO, a good fit for the integration), its head of sales (replaced after an eight-month gap), the IAR unit head, two heads of the testing unit (now interim, outside the management team) and its US sales leaders. The management team was expanded to ten after IAR. Former employees describe a rigid two-person (CEO and CFO) leadership model, a Finnish-centric structure, and a top-down culture in which key engineering and sales talent has left. Tuukka Turunen (R&D, 130,000 shares) and the long-tenured core engineers provide continuity. Pay disclosure for the rest of the team is only in aggregate (EUR 1.53 million in 2025).

What would change the view: a permanent, strong head of testing; a visible improvement in US sales under the new leaders; and a named succession plan.

C-58

How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

D5614th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: moderately well. Product investment has been protected (R&D at 15-20% of sales, AI tools launched on time, certifications maintained), and the balance sheet can carry the current debt. But the company was not built for resilience: it over-hired for growth in 2022-2024, carries no currency hedging, borrowed for IAR at the start of a downturn, and has now cut 18% of staff, which thins the buffer in sales and support.

Resources in place: about 317 product developers (2025); a 30-year code base; certifications for safety and security; pre-integrations with major chipmakers; direct sales in three regions; IAR's compiler portfolio and its 70 chip partners; cash generation that can repay debt in two years. Investment continued through the downturn: Clate, AI assistants, Qt 6.12 compliance, NVIDIA and Qualcomm work, and APAC hiring during the restructuring.

Gaps: (1) no hedging despite 62% foreign-currency revenue; (2) the cost base was set for 15-25% growth and had to be cut; (3) leadership vacancies in testing; (4) after the cuts, capacity to absorb a demand upturn may be limited in some areas, especially US sales and customer support; (5) the share price and the 2025-2027 share plan, almost certainly worthless, give little retention for key people.

C-60

How clear, straightforward and helpful is the company's communication, including on the strategy and the driver transmission mechanism?

C-5512th of 26

Judgement: candid and clear on direction and on failures, unhelpful on the numbers needed to model the business; communication has improved in 2026 (ARR, quarterly royalties, floor guidance), but the transmission from drivers to reported numbers still has to be inferred.

Helpful: the chief executive's plain speech (2025 "was not good"; the forecasting models "were broken"); clear explanations of mechanisms such as the effect of 1-year contracts on recognition and of the IAR transition (a EUR 5-10 million revenue reduction in 2026); quarterly ARR since Q4 2025 and quarterly distribution licence revenue since early 2026; an IR lead being hired.

C-61

How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?

Judgement: strong over the long term and strong in the last six months, with a weak 2025 in between. QT delivered huge growth in 2016-2024 and has acted fast and visibly since the October 2025 warning (reorganisation, IAR integration, security exit, subscription conversion ahead of plan, Clate on time, two quarters of beats), but it underperformed peers in the tougher 2025 environment, so its record is not one of outperforming in hard conditions.

Delivery: revenue up about fivefold from 2016 to 2024 with margins rising; the subscription transition delivered; testing tripled; IAR integrated within 12 months with 68% subscription bookings against a 40% plan; up to 200 roles cut on schedule (Finland concluded in June, the US by June, other countries through late 2026); Q2 and Q3 2026 revenue beat consensus by 8% and 7%.

C-62

C-5611th of 26

Outlier positives: almost no capitalised development (EUR 0.14 million in 2025) against peers that capitalise; workforce reduction of up to 200 roles, among the top 5% of Nordic software companies by intensity (Leadership report); IAR subscription bookings at 68% within a year against a 40% target; product launches on schedule; certification depth that rivals lack. Against rivals it has won: Qt is the default framework while Kanzi, EB GUIDE, Unity and Flutter's embedded effort faded.

C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

A681st of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: governance is clean and largely shareholder-aligned: modest and genuinely variable pay, demanding share plans, an independent board with a large owner as chair, one share one vote, no anti-takeover measures, and an exercised clawback. The weak points are a capital-return policy that has never returned cash, limited pay disclosure below the chief executive, and the absence of currency hedging as a prudential safeguard.

Evidence for shareholders first:

  • Pay. The chief executive's total pay fell from EUR 524,430 (2023) to 492,124 (2024) and 446,846 (2025, entirely fixed). The 2022-2024 share plan paid nothing because the revenue threshold was missed. The bonus is capped at 120% of base salary.
  • Alignment. The chief executive holds 400,982 shares (about EUR 12 million, roughly 27 times his 2025 pay); the chair's group holds 21.6% and bought shares in May 2026 (a token amount).
  • Board. Five, now six, members independent of the company; the audit committee fully independent; the nomination and compensation committee majority independent (the chair is not independent of the major shareholder). The board clawed back wrongly paid fees in 2025.
  • Structure. One share one vote; no poison pill; an auditor tender after KPMG's ten-year term; two signatures required for significant transactions; no material related-party transactions.
  • Prudence. Conservative accounting and guidance.

Against: (1) no dividend since 2016 and no buyback despite authorisation, with all cash directed to growth and M&A (2) pay of the rest of management disclosed only in aggregate; (3) no internal audit function (reviewed by the audit committee); (4) no currency hedging, which leaves earnings exposed in a way most European software peers avoid; (5) the chair's 10-year tenure and the board's limited software expertise.

C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

D5014th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

Judgement: a good operator-allocator in organic and small-acquisition terms, with one transformational deal (Qt from Nokia, at Digia) and two good small deals; not a dynamic repurchaser (never); and its one large deal at QT (IAR) earns a low return before synergies. The record is above average but not enlightened.

Record: the 2012 purchase of Qt from Nokia (under Digia) created most of the value in the company's history; froglogic (2021) and Axivion (2022) were small and returned well (testing revenue tripled to EUR 34.5 million by 2024); IAR (2025) was strategically sound but priced at about five times revenue, funded with debt at the start of a downturn, and now returns about 7-8% pre-tax by 2028 before synergies. Organic reinvestment, the main use of cash, has earned very high returns in good years.

Repurchases: none, even with authorisation for 2 million shares and a share price that fell 51% in 2025 and sat near 18-26 in early 2026 (when the 24-month-forward P/E was near its five-year low). No dividend ever. The Leadership report's verdict ("strong" capital allocation) rests on the deal record; on the buyback and return dimension the record is absent.

For the model: assume cash goes to debt repayment until about 2028, then to bolt-on M&A any buyback would be upside.

4c

Financial profile

B+634th of 268 answers
Judgement63C-4962C-5060C-5166C-5264C-5360C-5462C-7670
Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

B+634th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The underlying economics are excellent (software gross margins above 80%, near-zero capex, free cash flow above net income, returns on tangible capital far above 50%) but the reported numbers are at a cyclical and self-inflicted trough, and the path out runs through operating leverage that cuts both ways. The 2027 consensus (EBITA margin about 30%, EPS about EUR 1.96, +80%) is an economic restoration rather than a stretch: it roughly returns EPS to the underlying 2024 level after a 50% larger cost base was built and then trimmed. The quality issues are specific and explainable: upfront recognition that exaggerates revenue swings, the IAR acquisition's low return on capital, and a fixed cost base that management adjusts only with a lag. For the modeller: margins should recover to 27-31% in 2027 (centre about 29%) and 30-34% by 2028-2029 depending on organic growth; free cash flow should be about 1.0-1.1x net income with working capital at 22-25% of revenue; leverage disappears by 2028; and the main sensitivities are organic growth (about 3-4% of EPS per point) and the dollar (about 8-10% of 2027 EPS per 10%).

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

C+628th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: EPS is in a deep cyclical and self-inflicted trough (2025: EUR 1.25; 2026e: about 1.09 adjusted, much less reported) and is set to rebound sharply in 2027 (consensus 1.96, +80%) on revenue growth of only 8.5%; the rebound is mostly cost and transition mechanics and is durable as a step change, while the underlying growth rate after 2027 is low-to-mid teens EPS growth on high single-digit revenue growth.

History: EPS rose from EUR 0.53 (2020) to 2.26 (2024), a 44% compound rate on revenue growth of 16-52% a year; then fell 45% to 1.25 in 2025 on revenue growth of 3.5%, because costs had been built for 15-25% growth. The 2024 peak is flattered: pre-tax profit (70.4) exceeded EBIT (63.1) because of a EUR 6.7 million earn-out revaluation gain, so underlying 2024 EPS was about EUR 2.0, almost exactly the 2027 consensus.

Forecasts (FactSet median): EPS 1.09 / 1.96 / 2.42 / 3.11 for 2026-2029 on revenue growth of 12.4% / 8.5% / 7.8% / 5.3%. The 2026-2027 jump comes from: the end of EUR 9.7 million of restructuring costs (not in adjusted EPS); EUR 20 million of savings (partly visible from H2 2026); IAR moving from a subscription trough to growth; and lower interest as debt is repaid. 2028's +23% needs a further rise of about 3 points in EBIT margin on about 8% revenue growth.

Durability: the step change to about EUR 2 in 2027 is fairly durable because most of it is cost-driven and already executed; its size depends on how much of the saving is reinvested. Beyond 2027, a sustainable rate is revenue growth of 7-10% plus 1-2 points of margin leverage a year, giving EPS growth of about 12-18%, with currency and royalty cycles adding volatility of ±10% in any year.

C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

C608th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: the core business needs almost no capital (capex is about 0.5-1% of sales), so its growth is funded through the income statement (R&D and sales spend) at very high returns when demand is there; the scope to deploy balance-sheet capital at high returns is limited, and the one large deployment (IAR) earns below the cost of capital before synergies. This argues for a high fair multiple on the core earnings stream and a discount for capital allocation.

Operating spend: the highest-return uses are testing sales capacity (a fragmented market growing 15-20% a year), APAC sales (Chinese automotive momentum), US sales execution, and AI features that keep Qt the default under AI-generated code. Each euro of incremental licence revenue carries an incremental margin of 80-90%, so spending that lifts growth by one point of revenue (about EUR 2.6 million) pays back within a year if it costs less than about EUR 2 million.

Capital: free cash flow of EUR 54-63 million a year in 2027-2028 (consensus) will retire net debt by about mid-2028, after which QT will generate EUR 60-70 million a year with no internal use for it. IAR (EUR 205 million) earns, on segment consensus (EUR 47 million of revenue by 2028) and its historical margin, a pre-tax return of about 7-8% before cross-selling, which is below Inderes' 9.4% cost of capital. Future acquisitions are therefore the main risk to returns, and buybacks the main untested lever.

Fair multiple: a business that grows 8-12% with returns on tangible capital far above 50% and little reinvestment need merits a multiple well above the market's: Planisware, with similar growth and margins, trades on 23x earnings, and the stock's own ten-year median 24-month-forward P/E is 29x. The capital-allocation record (one low-return large deal, no buybacks) and cyclicality justify a discount to those levels, not the current 13-15x.

C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

B665th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: returns on tangible capital are exceptionally high (well above 50% after tax), returns on capital employed including goodwill have fallen to the mid-teens after IAR and should recover to the low-to-mid 20s pre-tax by 2027-2028, and the return on the newest invested capital (IAR) is in single digits.

  • Capital employed (including goodwill): ROCE 43.4% in 2024, 15.5% in 2025 after the IAR purchase. With capital employed of about EUR 300 million (equity of about EUR 237 million at end-2026 on consensus plus net debt of about EUR 70 million) and 2027 EBITA of about EUR 79 million, pre-tax ROCE would be about 26% and post-tax about 20%. The Business Foundations report's conditional view, a return to 30%-plus if IAR's conversion succeeds, needs 2028-level profits.
  • Tangible capital: net working capital of about EUR 58 million (Q3 2026), negligible fixed assets (capex EUR 1.1 million in 2025) and small leases; post-tax EBITA of about EUR 63 million in 2027 on roughly EUR 65-70 million of tangible capital gives a return of about 90-100%.
  • New invested capital: IAR, about 5-6% post-tax by 2028 before synergies (C-50); the earlier small acquisitions (froglogic, Axivion) earned well, since testing revenue tripled.

The Business Foundations weak signal, that the fall in ROCE "exceeds normal acquisition dilution", is fair: QT paid about five times IAR's revenue for a business it then deliberately shrank. The core economics remain excellent; the group return now depends on IAR's 2027-2028 recovery.

C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

B+644th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: conversion is high (free cash flow of 91-123% of net income in 2023-2025; consensus 1.6x, 1.1x and 1.0x for 2026-2028) with almost no capex; the item that moves it is working capital, which is unusually large for software (about 24% of revenue, peaking at Q4) because 95% of multi-year contract value is recognised upfront. Shorter contracts lower working capital and lift conversion; a return to 3-year deals would do the opposite.

Facts: operating cash flow EUR 40.0 million (2023), 53.7 million (2024), 40.2 million (2025), and 29.5 million in the first nine months of 2026 (32.4 million a year earlier), about 70-75% of EBITDA. Capex EUR 0.8-1.3 million a year. Net working capital EUR 57.8 million at Q3 2026, 23.8% of trailing revenue (Q4 2025 peak 29.5%); trade receivables EUR 50.5 million at Q3 2026. Q4 is the biggest invoicing quarter, with cash collected in the first half of the next year. Tax paid fell to EUR 6.3 million in the first nine months of 2026 from EUR 11.0 million.

Mechanism for the model: when QT signs a 3-year contract it books about 95% of three years' value at signing but collects it over the contract (contract assets); when customers switch to 1-year terms, contract assets run off (EUR 3.9 million less in 2025, as the former CFO explained). That is why free cash flow was 123% of net income in 2025, a year of weak profit, and why 2022, a year of strong multi-year bookings, had negative operating cash flow. So: model working capital at about 22-25% of revenue, falling if 1-year terms persist; restructuring cash outflow of about EUR 10 million in 2026; free cash flow of about 1.0-1.1x net income in 2027-2028. Note that FactSet's free cash flow does not deduct lease payments (about EUR 2-3 million a year) or acquisitions.

C-53

How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

C-6011th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: gross margins are high (above 80% on licences; near 100% on royalties) and stable; operating margins fell from 34.1% (EBITA, 2024) to 24.0% (2025) and about 21% underlying in 2026, and are now turning up, with consensus at about 30% EBITA in 2027 and 32% in 2028. The trend is up, but the 2027 level is the key uncertainty.

Gross margin: the economic gross margin is above 80%, royalties near 100%, consulting below 10% of revenue; IAR's subscription shift and testing growth do not dilute it. FactSet's gross margin line (33% in 2025, 22% in Q3 2026) classifies personnel into cost of sales and should not be used.

Operating margin, company EBITA basis: 2023 30.2%; 2024 34.1%; 2025 24.0%; 2026 Q1 9.6%, Q2 22.1% underlying, Q3 21.3% underlying; nine months about 18% underlying and 12.1% reported. Consensus (brokers' basis): EBITDA margin 23.9% / 32.1% / 34.5% / 35.8% for 2026-2029; EBIT margin (after acquired-intangible amortisation) about 16% / 25% / 28%; implied EBITA margin about 30% in 2027.

Drivers of the trend: revenue mix (royalties up or down moves margin by about 0.25 points per EUR 1 million at near-100% margin), the fixed personnel base (about 64% of operating costs), the savings and their partial reinvestment, IAR's return to growth, and currency (euro and krona costs against dollar revenue). Q4 is structurally the highest-margin quarter (Q4 2025: 35.5% EBITA).

Peers: Planisware's EBITDA margin is 37% and rising slowly; DNB Carnegie puts international software peers at about 30% EBIT. QT's pre-downturn 34% EBITA shows the model can reach peer margins; the question is when.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

B+625th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: the earnings base is clean on capitalisation and tax and has a large, explainable adjusted-to-reported gap in 2025-2026; the two items that flatter or distort it are upfront revenue recognition, which pulls revenue forward and makes growth lumpier, and one-off financial gains (2024).

Gaps: (1) Restructuring costs of EUR 9.7 million in the first nine months of 2026 (about 4% of revenue), described as finished; 2026 reported EPS was EUR 0.15 for nine months. (2) Acquisition costs of EUR 5.8 million in 2025. (3) Amortisation of acquired intangibles, about EUR 12.5 million a year after IAR (EUR 9.3 million in 2025), excluded from EBITA and from Inderes' EPS but included in the other brokers'. (4) In 2024, a EUR 6.7 million earn-out revaluation gain in financial income lifted EPS by about EUR 0.25.

Not flattering: capitalised development is almost nil (EUR 0.14 million in 2025) and IAR's capitalisation was stopped in 2026, which is conservative and makes QT's EBITDA comparable or better than peers'. The tax rate is a normal 19-21% (21% in 2025). Share count is stable at 25.4 million; the 2025-2027 share plan's maximum is 214,000 shares (0.8%), and it will probably pay nothing.

The non-obvious items: (a) Revenue recognition: 95% of multi-year subscription value is recognised at signing, so revenue rises with contract length and falls when customers shorten terms, independently of usage. This made 2025 look worse and could make 2026-2027 look better as annual renewals compound; the ARR line is the cleaner measure. (b) Unhedged currency revaluations of intercompany balances in financial items (a EUR 1.8 million loss in 2025). (c) Interest expense of about EUR 4-5 million in 2026, falling.

For the model, use the brokers' adjusted basis consistently, model amortisation explicitly at about EUR 12.5 million a year (declining slowly), and treat the 2024 EPS of 2.26 as about 2.0 underlying.

C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

C+707th of 26

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: financial leverage is modest and falling fast; it adds little EPS amplification or risk. Net debt of EUR 76.5 million (1.3-1.4x EBITDA) at Q3 2026 should fall to near zero by end-2027 or 2028 from free cash flow, and the Risks report's covenant scenario is not realistic at this leverage.

Facts: EUR 120 million bank loan plus EUR 6.6 million of leases; cash EUR 50.2 million; net gearing 35.9%; equity ratio about 54%; EUR 15 million repaid in H1 2026; consensus total debt EUR 80 million (end-2026), 40 million (end-2027), 1 million (end-2028). Net interest expense was EUR 1.3 million in 2025 and, on consensus (EBIT less pre-tax profit), about EUR 4.6 million in 2026, 4.2 million in 2027 and 4.0 million in 2028. That consensus path looks high once debt falls: on a declining balance at roughly 3-4%, interest should fall towards EUR 2-3 million in 2027 and EUR 1 million in 2028, a possible EPS upside of EUR 0.05-0.10 against consensus by 2028 (to be checked against the loan terms, which the files do not give).

Amplification: from 2026 to 2028, falling interest adds perhaps EUR 0.10-0.15 to EPS, about 5% of 2028 EPS: small. Risk: leverage would rise above 2x only if EBITDA fell about 40% from the 2026 level, which would take a combination of a royalty collapse, another year of near-zero organic growth and a much weaker dollar. The Risks report's view that a 20% dollar fall plus organic softness could breach covenants assumes the depressed 2025-2026 earnings base; at 2027 earnings it is remote.

For the model: no equity issuance, debt repaid from free cash flow, interest falling, and net cash from about 2028.

Pricing power and operating leverage (and others)

5a

Pricing power
